# PERMANENCY PLANNING SERVICES POLICY, PROTOCOL, AND GUIDANCE

## Purpose

Permanency Planning Services are provided to children who must be separated from their own parents or caretakers when they are unable or unwilling to provide adequate protection and care. As a result, the child enters the custody of a North Carolina County child welfare services agency. When this happens, the county child welfare services agency has legal custody and/or placement responsibility, whether the child has been removed from their home, and regardless of the type of placement.

County child welfare custody must not be considered until reasonable efforts have been made to preserve a child's safety, health, and well-being in their own home. County child welfare agencies are required to provide services to preserve or reunify families until parental rights have been terminated by the juvenile court.

When county child welfare custody is necessary, it is the responsibility of the county child welfare services agency to ensure the child remains in its custody for the shortest time possible. Permanency Planning Services require a thorough assessment of the child and family's needs and careful planning prior to and throughout a child's experience in county child welfare custody.

Permanency Planning Services must be provided to any child in the custody or placement responsibility of a county child welfare services agency. Permanency Planning Services include but are not limited to:

o Careful planning and decision making with the family about placement, when necessary, and preparing the child, the child's family, and the foster family for separation and placement, including developing a family time and contact (visitation) agreement; o Assessing children's needs to ensure appropriate placement and services; o Arranging and monitoring a placement appropriate to the child's needs; o Involving the kinship network to provide planning, placement, and other support for the child and family; o Assessing family strengths and needs to determine the appropriate plan for service; o Developing and arranging community-based services to support the child and family; o Collaborating with other community service providers working with the family to ensure continuity of services and to prevent duplication of services; o Referring the child and family to needed services, including clinical treatment; o Collaborating with educational agencies to ensure school stability for the child and that all factors relating to the child's best interest are considered in determining the child's educational setting; all appropriate educational services are provided to the child; and documentation of educational planning is in the case file; o Providing ongoing assessment to determine risk to the child and to guide the case planning process;

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 2 -->
o Working with the family to develop and implement the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement; o Helping the family meet the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement objectives by providing information, instruction, guidance, and mentoring related to parenting skills, and by monitoring and updating the agreement with the family; o Providing case planning and management; o Concurrent permanency planning with the family to develop alternative options to provide a permanent home for a child should reunification fail; o Supervising the placement to ensure the child receives proper care during placement; o Preparing for and participating in court proceedings; o Preparing for and facilitating Child and Family Team /Permanency Planning Review meetings; o Providing transportation for children in county child welfare agency custody when needed and not otherwise available, including visits with parents, siblings, and relatives; o Providing LINKS services to assist older youth in learning life skills necessary to make a successful transition from foster care to living on their own; o Ensuring placements across state lines comply with the Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children (ICPC); o Recruiting and assessing relatives and other kin as potential caregivers; o Involving foster parents in planning and decision making for children in county child welfare agency custody; o Preparing children for adoptive placements and maintaining life books; and o Maintaining the permanency planning case record and thorough documentation of case activities.

Foster care can be defined as a situation in which, for a period, a child lives with and is cared for by people who are not the child's parents. There are different forms of foster care, including but not limited to:

o Family foster homes, o Therapeutic foster homes, and o Residential care.

For child welfare, "foster care" refers to children in the legal custody of a county child welfare services agency; however, a child may also be placed in foster care by their parent without involvement of a county child welfare services agency.

In order to keep children in foster care connected to their family, it is important to consider the child's culture. Child welfare defines culture as "a set of learned values, beliefs, customs, traditions, and practices shared by a group of people and can be passed from one generation to another. This can include, but is not limited to: religion, ethnic customs, traditions of family and community."

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Medicaid- Children and Families Specialty Plan (Medicaid- CFSP)

| Legal Basis:<br>The legal basis for coordinating Medicaid with the healthcare needs of children in foster care is established in Titles IV-E and XIX of the Social Security<br>Act. Title IV-E, which governs federal payments for foster care and adoption assistance, requires states to develop a plan that includes safeguards for<br>the use of information about individuals receiving foster care assistance. Section 471(a)(8) permits the use of such information for the administration<br>of other federal or federally assisted programs, including Medicaid, thereby enabling data sharing and coordination between child welfare and health<br>agencies 42 U.S.C. § 671(a)(8). Additionally, the Fostering Connections to Success and Increasing Adoptions Act of 2008 (42 U.S.C. § 622(15)(A))<br>amended Title IV-B to require states to create a plan for the ongoing oversight and coordination of healthcare services for children in foster care,<br>explicitly including coordination with the state Medicaid agency.<br>Title XIX of the Social Security Act, which created the Medicaid program, further reinforces this linkage. Section 1902(a)(10)(A)(i)(I) mandates that<br>children receiving Title IV-E foster care assistance are automatically eligible for Medicaid, ensuring seamless access to healthcare services for this<br>vulnerable population 42 U.S.C. § 1396a(a)(10)(A)(i)(I). This statutory connection between foster care and Medicaid eligibility facilitates integrated<br>service delivery and supports the broader goal of improving health outcomes for children in foster care. Together, these provisions establish a clear<br>legal mandate for states to coordinate child welfare and Medicaid systems to meet the comprehensive healthcare needs of children in foster care. | |

|MEDICAID CHILDREN AND FAMILIES SPECIALTY PLAN (Medicaid- CFSP)<br>The Medicaid Children and Families Specialty Plan (Medicaid- CFSP) is a<br>North Carolina Medicaid managed care health plan. You may also hear<br>the Medicaid-CFSP referred to as Healthy Blue Care Together (HBCT) or<br>simply "Healthy Blue". The Medicaid-CFSP is designed to improve<br>continuity of care and coordination to better serve children, youth, and<br>families receiving child welfare services. Medicaid-CFSP is available to<br>children and youth currently and formerly in foster care, and covers a<br>full range of physical health, behavioral health, pharmacy, long-term<br>services and supports (LTSS), 1915(i) services, and<br>intellectual/developmental (I/DD) services under a statewide care<br>management system.<br>**Medicaid-CFSP Eligible populations:**<br>•<br>Children andyouth in foster care|CHILDREN AND FAMILIES SPECIALTY PLAN (Medicaid- CFSP)<br>**Medicaid-CFSP Care Manager Role:**<br>Medicaid-CFSP Care Managers provide consultation, education and<br>system navigation for County DSS support staff. Medicaid-CFSP care<br>managers offer regular, in-person or virtual availability to support<br>complex youth cases. Additional Medicaid-CFSP care manager<br>responsibilities include:<br>Coordinating and Overseeing Care<br>•<br>Serve as the primary point of contact for DSS Medicaid-<br>funded emergency placements and transitions.<br>•<br>Oversee services, appointments, and care plans to<br>ensure timely, coordinated care that meets each<br>member's needs.<br>•<br>Maintain up-to-date client information in NC CarePoints|

## Required Timeframes

| Required Timeframes | |
|---|---|
|• Date of Non-Secure Custody<br>Order|• Permanency Planning Services case begins|
|• Within 24 hours of placement|• Verification of Custody Letter given to placement provider and the juvenile's school<br>• GAL volunteers must be notified prior to a placement change occurring, unless there are emergency<br>circumstances (N.C.G.S. § 7B-903.1(d)).|
|• Within 3 days after the day of<br>placement|• Determination of Foster Care Assistance Benefits and/or Medical Benefits Only(DSS-5120) initiated|
|• Within 7 business days of<br>placement|• Determination of Foster Care Assistance Benefits and/or Medical Benefits Only(DSS-5120) completed<br>• Medicaid eligibility worker must be notified within 24 hours of any subsequent placement changes|
|• Within 7 calendar days of<br>placement|• Face-to-face visit with child(ren); this contact is in addition to any contact or interaction with the child(ren)<br>on the day of placement<br>• Face-to-face contact with placement provider (at least one adult caretaker) in the provider's home; this is in<br>addition to any contact or interaction with the placement provider(s) on the day of placement<br>• Family Time (visitation) for child(ren) with parent(s)/caretaker(s) and sibling(s)<br>• Complete the following:<br>`o` Face-to-face contact with parent(s)/caretaker(s)<br>`o` Initial Health Screening (Health Summary Form - Initial Visit)(DSS-5206) <br>`o` Educational Stability addressed (Child Education Status) (DSS-5245) or NC Best Interest Determination<br>Form (DSS-5137) completed) including BID meeting (within 5 school days) prior to any school change<br>`o` SIS Client Entry Form(DSS-5027)<br>`o` Child Placement and Payment System Report(DSS-5094)|
|• Within 14 calendar days of<br>placement|• Shared Parenting meeting<br>• Family Time and Contact Plan(DSS-5242) developed jointly with parent(s)<br>• Family Time and Contact Plan developed for siblings to visit each other (if in separate placements)(DSS-<br>5242)|

# Required Timeframes

| Required Timeframes | |
|---|---|
|• Within 30 calendar days of<br>placement|• Develop the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement(DSS-5240)<br>• Develop the Transitional Living Plan (TLP) for any child 14 years old or older<br>• Make diligent efforts to identify and notify adult relatives and other persons with legal custody of a sibling of<br>the juvenile. When it is safe and appropriate children must be placed with relatives.<br>• All information about diligent efforts to locate must be documented and filed with the court.<br>• Complete the following:<br>`o` Relative Notifications [e.g., via Relative Notification Letter(DSS-5317) <br>`o` Health Summary Form - 30-day Comprehensive Visit(DSS-5208)<br>`o` Health History Form(DSS-5207), and provide copies to the placement provider<br>`o` Review and update (if needed) the Child Education Status(DSS-5245) <br>`o` Review of the Family Assessment of Strengths and Needs(DSS-5229) <br>`o` Review of the Risk Assessment(DSS-5230)<br>• Begin the child(ren)'s life book|
|• Within 60 calendar days of<br>placement|• Initial Permanency Planning Review and complete the following:<br>`o` Permanency Planning Review(DSS-5241)<br>`o` Family Strengths and Needs Assessment(DSS-5229)<br>`o` Family Reunification Assessment(DSS-5227) <br>`o` Review and update (if needed) the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement(DSS-5240) <br>• Adjudicatory Hearing (within 60 days of the filing of the petition, unless the judge orders it be held later)|
|• Within 90 days of the 60-day<br>CFT/PPR (150 calendar days of<br>placement), and every 90 days<br>thereafter throughout the life of<br>the case|• Permanency Planning Review and complete the following:<br>`o` Permanency Planning Review(DSS-5241)<br>`o` Review and update the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement(DSS-5240)<br>`o` Family Strengths and Needs Assessment(DSS-5229)<br>`o` Family Reunification Assessment(DSS-5227)|

# Case Staffing / Two-Level Decision Making / Role of Supervisor

# Protocol and Guidance

# Case Staffing/Two-Level Decision Making/Role of Supervisor

|Policy|Guidance|
|---|---|
|CASE STAFFING / TWO-LEVEL DECISION MAKING /<br>ROLE OF SUPERVISOR<br>The county child welfare supervisor and assigned county child welfare<br>worker must staff each permanency planning case:<br>•<br>At least once a month throughout the life of the case; and<br>•<br>Whenever there is a change in circumstance that impacts safety<br>and/or permanency of the child.<br>At least monthly, staffing must cover but not be limited to:<br>•<br>Safety, well-being, and permanence;<br>•<br>Family's strengths and needs;<br>•<br>Discussion of juvenile's placement if not placed with siblings and<br>efforts that have been made to place the juvenile siblings together;<br>•<br>Family's progress; and<br>•<br>Review of the ongoing family and collateral contacts.<br>Two-level decisions/reviews must occur on every permanency<br>planning case at the following times:<br>•<br>At the time of the development and required reviews of the<br>Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement;<br>•<br>At required PPR meetings;<br>•<br>Whenever there is a potential placement disruption and/or<br>placement change;<br>•<br>Whenever there is a change in circumstance that impacts the safety<br>or risk to a child(ren);<br>•<br>The child is an abducted or runaway child;|CASE STAFFING<br>Case staffing can occur in various forms. The focus of case staffing is to<br>ensure that the case county child welfare worker follows North Carolina<br>child welfare policy, addresses child and family needs, and monitors:<br>risk, safety, and family progress. Supervision provides coaching and<br>support to the county child welfare worker. Achieving these goals may<br>be accomplished through an office meeting but could also occur when a<br>supervisor attends a home visit or other family meeting with a county<br>child welfare worker. Participation in the case CFT/PPR meeting by both<br>the child welfare (CW) caseworker and the supervisor could be<br>considered as a staffing during that month.<br>TWO-LEVEL DECISION MAKING / ROLE OF SUPERVISOR<br>Two-level decisions for Permanency Planning Services cases should<br>involve the assigned county child welfare worker and that worker's<br>supervisor. However, there may be circumstances that require another<br>county child welfare worker or another supervisor or a higher-level<br>manager in the agency to participate in the decision making.<br>The child welfare supervisor should review every permanency planning<br>electronic NC FAST record at least quarterly and prior to case closure.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

## Required Forms: Planning Forms

## Required Forms: Planning Forms

Legal Basis Fostering Connections to Success and Increasing Adoptions Act of 2008, P.L. 110-351 requires county child welfare agencies to exercise due diligence to notify all close adult relatives of a child (including any other adult relatives suggested by the parents) within 30 days of the child's removal from the parent of their options to participate in the care and placement of the child.

N.C.G.S. 7B-505 (b)Requires the department of social services to use due diligence to identify and notify adult relatives and other persons with legal custody of a sibling of the juvenile within 30 days after the initial order removing custody. The county social services agency must file with the court all information regarding attempts made to identify and notify adult relatives of the juvenile and persons with legal custody of a sibling of the juvenile. In placing a juvenile in nonsecure custody under this section, the court shall first consider whether a relative of the juvenile is willing and able to provide proper care and supervision of the juvenile in a safe home. If the court finds that the relative is willing and able to provide proper care and supervision in a safe home, then the court shall order placement of the juvenile with the relative unless the court finds that placement with the relative would be contrary to the best interests of the juvenile.

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|RELATIVE NOTIFICATION<br>County child welfare agencies must identify and notify all adult relatives<br>and other persons with legal custody of a sibling of the child within 30<br>days of the child's removal from their parent(s)/caretaker(s). County child<br>welfare agencies must provide documentation of those efforts to the<br>court.<br>The county child welfare agency must thoroughly search for relatives, and<br>when it is safe and appropriate juvenile children must be placed with<br>relatives.<br>Relative is defined as an individual directly related to the juvenile by<br>blood, marriage, or adoption. This includes but is not limited to the<br>following examples: grandparents, siblings, aunts, and uncles.|Notification to relatives is subject to exceptions due to family<br>circumstances such as domestic violence.<br>County child welfare agencies should provide to the court efforts made to<br>contact relatives and/or a legal custodian of a sibling and include the date<br>the notification was sent, the response from the notification, and the<br>reasons why the relative or legal custodian of a sibling was or was not<br>recommended for placement by the agency. The local county agency<br>should also provide copies of all correspondence with relatives and/or a<br>legal custodian of a sibling in the case file.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|Adoptive parents of siblings are not considered relatives of the legal child;<br>however, they must be considered in the identification and notification of<br>individuals who have legal custody of a sibling.<br>_Per federal law, individuals are considered siblings of a child even_<br>_if termination or disruption of parental rights, such as death, has_<br>_occurred._<br>The county child welfare agency must make diligent efforts to notify the<br>following people within 30 days after the initial order removing custody:<br>•<br>Adult relatives and kin suggested by parents;<br>•<br>Adult maternal and paternal grandparents, aunts, uncles, siblings,<br>great grandparents, nieces, and nephews;<br>•<br>All parents of a sibling where such parent has legal custody of<br>such sibling; and<br>•<br>Relatives and other persons with legal custody of a sibling.<br>The following are the relative notification forms generated through NC<br>FAST:<br>•<br>Relative Notification Letter (DSS-5317) <br>•<br>Relative Interest Form (DSS-5316)<br>NOTIFICATION REQUIREMENTS<br>•<br>The following must be included in relative notifications:<br>•<br>That the child has been removed from the custody of the parent;<br>•<br>The options the relative has under federal, state, and local law to<br>participate in the care and placement of the child;<br>•<br>The options that may be lost by failing to respond to the notice;<br>•<br>The requirements to become a family foster home;|TheRelative Search Informationform (DSS-5318) should also be used to<br>document relative information and subsequently scanned and attached in<br>the electronic record.<br>The purpose of relative notification is to ensure adult relatives of children<br>under the care and supervision of county child welfare agencies are given<br>the consideration and opportunity to be placement resources and/or to<br>be able to participate in the child's care plan.<br>The Relative Notification Letter and the Relative Interest Form should be<br>sent together to identified relatives and kin because these forms<br>complement each other. The Relative Search Information Form should<br>also be sent with the Relative Notification letter to obtain additional<br>relative information.<br>•<br>Efforts to notify should include, but are not limited to:<br>•<br>Interviewing the child and the child's parents or caretakers about<br>the child's relatives and their preferences for placement;<br>•<br>Using family decision-making meetings such as Child and Family<br>Team (CFT) meetings to ask participants to help identify other<br>relatives of the child;<br>•<br>Contacting identified relatives and requesting names of other<br>relatives, divulging only information necessary to help identify<br>additional relatives and assess their interest in accepting<br>placement of the child or providing connections;<br>•<br>Accessing the services history in NC FAST or internal county<br>agency databases such as child welfare and child support; and<br>•<br>Utilizing internet-based search tools, including social media.|

|PERMANENCY PLANNING / OUT OF HOME FAMILY SERVICES AGREEMENT<br>The Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement (DSS-5240)<br>generated through NC FAST, serves as the framework on which the<br>agency's work with the family and child is based and drives the agency's<br>work with the family.<br>The Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement must document the<br>objectives and action steps that the family, agency and other resources<br>will take while working to achieve permanency for the child.<br>The purpose of the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement<br>planning process is to:<br>•<br>Clarify with the family reasons for county child welfare services<br>agency involvement;<br>•<br>Identify resources within the family that will help the child<br>achieve a safe, permanent home;<br>•<br>Involve the family in identifying areas that need improvement;<br>•<br>Clarify expectations for behavioral change with all persons<br>involved; and<br>•<br>Acknowledge the family's strengths and commitment to their<br>child.<br>The development of the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement<br>must include:|The Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement should involve the<br>child and family during planning, and recognizes each family has strengths<br>and is unique. Services to the family should be collaborative and<br>community-based.<br>The Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement is a living document<br>that can be revised and updated as often as needed based on the<br>circumstances of the case.<br>Good case planning, using the either the PPR Review and/or the<br>Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement, should include the<br>problems which led to agency intervention, as well as establish goals<br>directed toward achieving safety, well-being, and permanence for the<br>child within a 12-month period.<br>The Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement should reflect both<br>the strengths and needs of the child and family identified in the Family<br>Assessment of Strengths and Needs and the Family Reunification<br>Assessment. The Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement should<br>document what must change for the parents to meet the needs of the<br>child.<br>The county child welfare worker should immediately engage the family,<br>both maternal and paternal, in the planning process, which is focused on<br>correcting the conditions that caused the county child welfare services<br>agency to be involved in the family.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|TRANSITIONAL LIVING PLAN<br>Purpose and Overview<br>The Social Security Act (42 U.S.C. § 675) requires states to have a plan for<br>foster care, which must include a Transitional Living Plan (TLP) to prepare<br>youth for independent living. Furthermore, the Fostering Connections to<br>Success and Increasing Adoptions Act of 2008 (P.L. 110351) expanded<br>foster care eligibility to age 21 in participating states and required that no<br>more than 90 days before a youth's exit from the foster care system, a TLP<br>must be developed with the youth a specific plan for his or her transition<br>to independent living.<br>Transitional Living Plan (TLP) Requirements<br>TLPs are required for youth in foster care beginning at age 14 and are<br>updated regularly to reflect the youth's evolving goals, needs, and<br>circumstances. TLPs are developed collaboratively with the youth and their<br>support network and are grounded in the results of a Life Skills<br>Assessment. TLP must be developed, reviewed, and updated according to<br>specific timelines.<br>**Transitional Living Plan for 14-17 Year Olds**<br>For youth ages 14-17 in foster care, a TLP (DSS-5096a) must be<br>developed within 30 days (and reviewed within 60 days) following the<br>youth's 14th birthday, or when the youth enters foster care, if age 14 or<br>older; and updated every 90 days thereafter. See Instructions Help Sheet<br>(DSS-5096ains) for more information.|<br>A Transitional Living Plan (TLP) is specifically designed with youth ages 14<br>to 17 who are in the custody of the county child welfare services agency.<br>This plan is jointly developed between the agency, youth, placement<br>provider, youth supporters, and others who are involved with the youth.<br>It is based on a life skills assessment and should directly target those<br>areas that need to be addressed before the youth becomes an adult. For<br>more about the life skills assessment seeNC LINKS.<br>The 90 Day TLP should be personalized at the direction of the youth, be as<br>detailed as he or she chooses, and include: specific options regarding how<br>to access housing; health insurance; education; local opportunities for<br>mentoring services and continuing services; sexual health services; and<br>resources to ensure the youth is informed and prepared to make healthy<br>living decisions about their lives.<br>While the TLP is developed to address independent living needs of the<br>youth while in care, the 90 Day Transition Plan for Youth in Foster Care is<br>considered an extension of the TLP because it provides details and<br>resources for the youth after he or she exits care.<br>The Transitional Living Plan - Helpful Resources for Young Adults should<br>include contact information for resources within the youth's community.<br>Information from the youth regarding their plans upon exiting care as<br>well as contact information should also be obtained.|

Data from county child welfare agencies is scrutinized<br>to identify strengths in the child welfare system and areas for<br>program improvement. The accuracy of data is critical in this<br>analysis. ## Domestic Violence and Permanency Planning

Protocol - What you must do

DOMESTIC VIOLENCE AND PERMANENCY PLANNING Assessment for the presence of domestic violence and its impact on the safety of children must occur throughout Permanency Planning Services.

Careful consideration must be given to cases involving domestic violence when:

• Developing the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement; • Developing and implementing the Family Time and Contact Plan; • Scheduling and holding CFT Meetings and PPR meetings; • Planning Shared Parenting meetings; and • Assessing the family for reunification.

For more information, please see "Domestic Violence" in Cross Function Topics in the NC Child Welfare manual.

## Safe Surrender and Permanency Planning

|Policy|Guidance|
|---|---|
|Safe Surrender<br>The General Assembly during the 2023 legislative session in accordance with<br>Senate Bill 20established this Article to protect newborn infants by providing a<br>safe alternative for a parent who, in a crisis or in desperation, may physically<br>abandon or harm his or her newborn and to provide information for the<br>parent regarding the parent's rights and alternatives.<br>G.S. 7B-101(19a) states that a safely surrendered infant is an infant reasonably<br>believed to be not more than 30 days of age and without signs of abuse or<br>neglect who is voluntarily delivered by a parent to one of the following and the<br>parent does not express an intent to return for the infant:<br>•<br>health care provider that is on duty or at a hospital, health<br>department, or community health center<br>•<br>first responder, including law enforcement officer, certified emergency<br>medical services worker, or firefighter<br>•<br>social services worker on duty or at a local department of social<br>services.<br>Once it has been determined that the infant meets the criteria for a safely<br>surrender infant by the assessment worker, coordination between the<br>assessment worker and the permanency planning worker must occur to<br>ensure services are not duplicated and appropriate services are in place. Per<br>Senate Bill 425, after notice by publication has been initiated, **the county may**<br>**apply for an ex-parte order to the court for the purposes of:**<br>•<br>Getting a court finding that the infant was safely surrendered and<br>•<br>Confirmation that the county has legal custody to be able to obtain the<br>birth certificate, social security, and the necessary benefits needed by<br>the infant.<br>The Permanency Planning worker must notify the placement provider of the<br>potential for the surrendering parent(s) to seek to regain custody prior to filing<br>of termination ofparental rights.|Refer to required timeframes and policy for case responsibilities<br>when a juvenile is placed in custody.<br>The county should ensure the Assessment worker and the<br>Permanency Planning worker are engaged and working together to<br>complete case requirements such as medical evaluations and<br>securing a safe and appropriate placement. The Permanency<br>Planning for a safely surrendered infant does not alter any of the<br>requirements for the permanency planning process. Therefore, the<br>permanency planning worker should carry out the same<br>responsibilities as with other permanency related cases.|

## Human Trafficking

Protocol - What you must do

HUMAN TRAFFICKING

A child who is sold, traded, or exchanged for sex or labor is an abused and neglected juvenile, regardless of the relationship between the victim and the perpetrator. County child welfare agencies must continue to assess the safety and well-being of children during the provision of Permanency Planning Services, including children who are suspected or confirmed to be victims of human trafficking.

County child welfare agencies must document in the electronic NC FAST records and determine appropriate services for children who are believed to be or are at risk of being victims of human trafficking.

County child welfare workers must follow North Carolina child welfare policy and practice on Human Trafficking.

County child welfare workers must complete the Human Trafficking Screening Tool (HTST) DSS-5402 for all youth upon return from runaway status within 24-hours.

For additional information see "Human Trafficking" in Cross Function Topics in the NC Child Welfare manual.

<!-- page: 45 -->
## Agency Plan for Missing, Abducted, and Runaway Children

PERMANENCY PLANNING SERVICES

## Agency Plan for Missing, Abducted, and Runaway Children

AGENCY PLAN FOR MISSING, ABDUCTED, AND RUNAWAY CHILDREN

Legal Basis

The Preventing Sex Trafficking and Strengthening Families Act (P.L. 113-183) requires agencies to identify, document, and determine appropriate services for any child or youth in the placement, care, or supervision of a county child welfare services agency who is at-risk of becoming a sex trafficking victim or who is a sex trafficking victim. Agencies must report immediately (no later than 24 hours) to law enforcement children or youth who the agency identifies as being a sex trafficking victim. Agencies must also have protocols in place to locate children missing from foster care, determine the factors that lead to the child's being absent from foster care, and determine the child's experiences while absent from foster care, including whether the child is a sex trafficking victim. Agencies must report immediately (no later than 24 hours) to law enforcement any missing or abducted children for entry into the National Crime Information Center (NCIC) database, and to the National Center for Missing and Exploited Children.

Public Law 117-348 - Trafficking Victims Prevention and Protection Reauthorization Act of 22 reauthorizes and amends the Trafficking Victims Protection Act (TVPA). Amendments include additional communication and details when title IV-E agencies report missing or abducted children to law enforcement and the National Center for Missing and Exploited Children (NCMEC).

Each county child welfare services agency must follow the policy below for reporting, locating, and returning children missing, runaway, or abducted from foster care.

Definitions

Abduction of Children - Per N.C.G.S. § 14-41 any person who, without legal justification or defense, abducts or induces any minor child who is at least four years younger than the person, to leave any person, agency, or institution lawfully entitled to the child's custody, placement, or care.

High Risk Child - The missing child's safety is severely compromised for one or more of the following reasons:

• The child is believed to be or has been abducted; • The child is believed to be in the company of adults who could endanger their safety; • The child is younger than 13 years of age and/or is believed to be out of the zone of safety for their age or developmental stage; • The child has one or more health conditions that, if not treated daily, will place the child at severe risk; • The child is drug dependent, including prescribed medication and/or illegal substances, and the dependency is life-threatening; • The child has severe emotional problems that, if not treated, will place the child at severe risk; • The child has a developmental disability that impairs the child's ability to care for him/herself; • The child is pregnant and/or parenting and the infant/child is believed to be with their parent(s);

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

## Agency Plan for Missing, Abducted, and Runaway Children

PERMANENCY PLANNING SERVICES

• The child is missing more than 24 hours before being reported to law enforcement; • The child is believed to be in a life-threatening situation; • The child's absence is inconsistent with their established patterns of behavior and the deviation is not readily explained; • The child is known or believed to be a victim of human trafficking (sex trafficking, labor trafficking, or both); and/or, • Other circumstances involved in the disappearance that would cause a reasonable person to conclude that the child should be considered "at imminent risk."

Kidnapping - Per N.C.G.S. § 14-39 any person who shall unlawfully confine, restrain, or remove from one place to another, any other person 16 years of age or over without the consent of such person, or any other person under the age of 16 years without the consent of a parent or legal custodian of such person.

Missing Child - Per N.C.G.S. § 143B-1011, a juvenile as defined in N.C.G.S § 7B-101 whose location has not been determined, who has been reported as missing to a law enforcement agency, and whose parent's, spouse's, guardian's, or legal custodian's temporary or permanent residence is in North Carolina or is believed to be in North Carolina.

Missing Foster Child - A child who has either left voluntarily (ran away) or involuntarily (abduction, kidnapped, or lost), and cannot be accounted for by the agency responsible for their care and placement.

Runaway - A child who has voluntarily left their placement provider or home and whose whereabouts are either unknown by the child's parent, guardian, custodian, caretaker, foster parent, or county child welfare worker or whose whereabouts are unknown by the child's parent, guardian, custodian, caretaker, foster parent, or county child welfare worker but who maintains periodic contact with the county child welfare worker or others.

|Guidance - Howyou should do it|Guidance - Howyou should do it|
|PREVENTING CHILDREN AND YOUTH FROM RUNNING AWAY<br>Prevention efforts on the part of county child welfare agencies should include, at a minimum, the following activities:<br>•<br>Maintaining administrative oversight of its program and practice to ensure agency compliance with laws and policies concerning case<br>contacts, reviews, and hearings;<br>•<br>Maintaining photographs of foster children in the NC FAST electronic record;<br>•<br>Assuring supervisory review and staffing of all permanency planning cases on a regular schedule;<br>•<br>Recruiting and supporting foster homes in communities from which foster children come into care;<br>•<br>Training foster parents regarding the dynamics of human trafficking cases and the specific needs of children and youth who have been<br>trafficked;<br>•<br>Educating individual foster parents and placement providers regarding the specific needs of the child or youth for whom they are providing<br>care;|PREVENTING CHILDREN AND YOUTH FROM RUNNING AWAY<br>Prevention efforts on the part of county child welfare agencies should include, at a minimum, the following activities:<br>•<br>Maintaining administrative oversight of its program and practice to ensure agency compliance with laws and policies concerning case<br>contacts, reviews, and hearings;<br>•<br>Maintaining photographs of foster children in the NC FAST electronic record;<br>•<br>Assuring supervisory review and staffing of all permanency planning cases on a regular schedule;<br>•<br>Recruiting and supporting foster homes in communities from which foster children come into care;<br>•<br>Training foster parents regarding the dynamics of human trafficking cases and the specific needs of children and youth who have been<br>trafficked;<br>•<br>Educating individual foster parents and placement providers regarding the specific needs of the child or youth for whom they are providing<br>care;|

|CHOOSING THE BEST PLACEMENT RESOURCE<br>(See also "Placement Preparation and Follow-Up" in Cross Function Topics in<br>theNC Child Welfare manual.) <br>The North Carolina Division of Social Services and county departments of<br>social services are prohibited from discriminating for reasons of race,<br>ethnicity, color, national origin, sex (including on the basis of sexual<br>orientation and on the basis of gender identity), religion, age, disability,<br>and/or political beliefs. This includes when working with youth, families,<br>parents, caregivers, kinship providers, adoptive parents, and licensed foster<br>parents.<br>PREVENTING PLACEMENT DISRUPTION<br>Placement disruptions must be documented in the electronic record for both<br>the child and foster parent's record.<br>When a CPS report involves an allegation against a placement provider and<br>the child is placed in the home at the time of the report, the child must only<br>be removed from the home prior to the case decision if a safety threat to the<br>child exists. If the child remains in the home, the county child welfare<br>services agency must develop and monitor a Safety Plan that ensures safety<br>until the case decision is made.|PREVENTING PLACEMENT DISRUPTION<br>The county child welfare services agency should have a plan to<br>manage placement disruption. Documenting the disruption in both<br>the child's record and the foster parent's record can provide valuable<br>information as to what kinds of behaviors the foster parent cannot<br>handle. This analysis can guide future placement decisions, as well as<br>identify training needs of foster parents.<br>One of the best tools to manage placement disruption is to plan for<br>placement supports such as regular and consistent respite care. The<br>county child welfare worker should have sufficient contact with the<br>placement provider to know when conditions exist that could lead to<br>disruptions. Intensive Family Preservation Services, as available, are<br>also a resource for preventing placement disruptions.|

|The Fostering Connections to Success and Increasing Adoptions Act of 2008, P.L. 110-351requires that agencies make reasonable efforts to place<br>siblings removed from their home in the same foster care, adoptive, or guardianship placement, unless it is contrary to the safety or<br>well-being of any of the siblings to do so.<br>N.C. G.S. 7B-505 and N.C. G.S. 7B-903.1 (c1) states**i**f juvenile siblings are removed from the home and placed in the nonsecure custody of a county<br>department of social services, the director shall make reasonable efforts to place the juvenile siblings in the same home. The director is not required<br>to make reasonable efforts if there is documentation that placing the juvenile siblings together would be contrary to any of the sibling's safety or well-<br>being. If, after making reasonable efforts, the director is unable to place the juvenile siblings in the same home, the director shall make reasonable<br>efforts to provide frequent sibling visitation and ongoing interaction between the juvenile siblings, unless the director documents that frequent<br>visitation or other ongoing interaction between the juvenile siblings would be contrary to the safety or well-being.|The Fostering Connections to Success and Increasing Adoptions Act of 2008, P.L. 110-351requires that agencies make reasonable efforts to place<br>siblings removed from their home in the same foster care, adoptive, or guardianship placement, unless it is contrary to the safety or<br>well-being of any of the siblings to do so.<br>N.C. # Placement Decision Making: Maintaining One Single, Stable Placement

| After adjudication, N.C.G.S. 7B-903:<br>(a1) In placing a juvenile in out-of-home care under this section, the court shall first<br>consider whether a relative of the juvenile is willing and able to provide proper care<br>and supervision of the juvenile in a safe home. If the court finds that the relative is<br>willing and able to provide proper care and supervision in a safe home, then the court<br>shall order placement of the juvenile with the relative unless the court finds that the<br>placement is contrary to the best interests of the juvenile. In placing a juvenile in out-<br>of-home care under this section, the court shall also consider whether it is in the<br>juvenile's best interest to remain in the juvenile's community of residence. Placement<br>of a juvenile with a relative outside of this State must be in accordance with the<br>Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children.<br>(a2) An order under this section placing or continuing the placement of the juvenile in<br>out-of-home care shall contain a finding that the juvenile's continuation in or return to<br>the juvenile's own home would be contrary to the juvenile's health and safety.<br>(a3) An order under this section placing the juvenile in out-of-home care shall contain<br>specific findings as to whether the department has made reasonable efforts to prevent<br>the need for placement of the juvenile. In determining whether efforts to prevent the<br>placement of the juvenile were reasonable, the juvenile's health and safety shall be the<br>paramount concern.<br>(a4) If the court does not place the juvenile with a relative, the court may consider<br>whether nonrelative kin or other persons with legal custody of a sibling of the juvenile<br>are willing and able to provide proper care and supervision of the juvenile in a safe<br>home. The court may order the department to notify the juvenile's State-recognized<br>tribe of the need for custodial care for the purpose of locating relatives or nonrelative kin<br>for placement. # Placement Decision Making: Maintaining One Single, Stable Placement

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|KINSHIP CARE (RELATIVE AND NON-RELATIVE KIN)<br>County child welfare agencies must strive to strengthen and preserve the family.<br>Parents must be given a reasonable opportunity to identify and come together<br>with their kinship network to plan for and provide safety, care, nurture, and<br>supervision for the child.See the following definitions (N.C.G.S. § 7B-101):<br>•<br>Relative. - An individual directly related to the juvenile by<br>blood, marriage, or adoption, including, but not limited to, a<br>grandparent, sibling, aunt, or uncle.<br>•<br>Nonrelative kin. - An individual having a substantial relationship with<br>the juvenile. In the case of a juvenile member of a State-recognized tribe<br>as set forth in G.S. 143B-407(a), nonrelative kin also includes any member<br>of a State-recognized tribe or a member of a federally recognized tribe,<br>whether or not there is a substantial relationship with the juvenile.<br>The county child welfare services agency must assess the suggested resource to<br>ensure the child will receive appropriate care and must give preference to an<br>adult relative or other kin when determining placement, provided that:<br>•<br>The placement is assessed by the county child welfare services agency to be<br>in the best interest of the child in terms of both safety and nurture; and<br>•<br>The prospective kinship care provider and the living situation are<br>assessed and determined to meet relevant standards.<br>The agency must first address available and appropriate relatives and whether a<br>relative is willing and able to provide proper care and supervision for the child. If<br>the home is assessed to be appropriate, placement must be approved by the<br>court and designated in the court order.<br>Adoptive parents of siblings are not considered relatives of the legal child;<br>however, they must be considered in the identification and notification of<br>individuals who have legal custody of a sibling. Placement with a legal custodian<br>of a sibling must be considered after all relatives have been excluded. If that|<br>Whether licensed as a foster home or not, kinship care providers<br>should be valued and treated as partners with the birth family and<br>the county child welfare services agency. This includes notifying<br>relatives and non-relative kin providing care for a child of any court<br>review or hearing to be held about the child and of their<br>opportunity to be heard in court regarding the juvenile's well-being.<br>County child welfare workers should use family-centered practice<br>tools, such as CFT meetings. CFT meetings provide a model for<br>engaging the kinship network at the earliest stages of agency<br>involvement.<br>The family's understanding incorporates a historical perspective of<br>the problems faced by the family, as well as the efforts to remedy<br>those problems. They can confront the problems and to help<br>provide realistic supports to help the child and their family of origin<br>move toward healing.<br>The county child welfare services agency should engage with the<br>members of the kinship network and share responsibility for<br>planning. This helps the family, the relatives, and other kin to take<br>ownership of the family's needs, to bring their own resources to<br>address those needs, to reduce the likelihood of child placement<br>outside the kinship network, and to provide a system of oversight<br>to the family's progress in the resolution of the issues.|

|OTHER PLACEMENT RESOURCES<br>If a relative or non-relative kin cannot be identified as an appropriate placement<br>resource for the child, a licensed foster care placement must be chosen for the<br>child that ensures the child is placed in the least restrictive, most family-like<br>setting available that best meets the needs of the child.<br>Licensed foster care placement resources must be carefully evaluated and<br>prepared prior to placement to help assure the child will remain in that<br>placement until reunification or another permanent home is achieved.<br>Pursuant to the Foster Parent's Bill of Rights, the county child welfare services<br>agency shall provide licensed foster parents any known information to help the<br>foster parents better care for children, including health and behavioral health<br>history, disability, exposure to trauma, and educational needs.<br>County child welfare services agencies shall also provide any known information<br>to help the kinship parents better care for children, including health and<br>behavioral health history, disability, exposure to trauma, and educational needs.<br>Licensed foster care placement resources include:<br>•<br>A foster family home or group home supervised by the county child<br>welfare services agency and licensed by the NC Department of Health<br>and Human Services;<br>•<br>A child-caring institution licensed or approved by the NC Department of<br>Health and Human Services and in compliance with Title VI of the Civil<br>Rights Act, Multiethnic Placement Act of 1994 (MEPA), as amended in<br>1996 by the Interethnic Placement provisions (MEPA-IEP);<br>•<br>A foster care facility under the auspices of a licensed or approved private<br>child care or child-placing agency; such foster care facilities must be<br>licensed by the NC Department of Health and Human Services and in<br>compliance with Title VI of the Civil Rights Act, Multiethnic Placement|If there are no relatives or non-relative kin who are willing and/or<br>able to care for the child long-term, the foster family should be<br>considered the first alternative for permanent placement through<br>adoption.<br>Placement providers are bringing a child into their home that they<br>may have no prior connection to or knowledge about. For the<br>initial transition to be successful, county child welfare agencies<br>should provide any known information about the child that would<br>be necessary for the care of the child, both physical and<br>psychological. At the time of placement, the placement provider<br>should be provided with any additional information that may have<br>been discovered during the removal, if it is relevant to the care of<br>the child. A child's trauma and the impact of the trauma may be<br>shared with the placement provider, but the specific details and<br>story of the trauma should not be shared. For example, it would<br>only be appropriate to share that a child's trauma is physical abuse,<br>and the impact of the trauma is that the child will only take a<br>shower and that the child has an aversion to cooking utensils.|

# Placement Decision Making: Maintaining One Single, Stable Placement

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|RAPID RESPONSE TEAM (RRT)<br>H612, section 1.16, amending N.C.G.S. 7B-903 (effective 6/26/2025)states if a<br>juvenile (i) is in the custody of the department of social services, (ii) requires<br>mental health treatment, and (iii) is present in the hospital by any means (with<br>an exception for those children who are under an effective involuntary<br>commitment or a voluntary admission order), certain steps must be taken:<br>1. The hospital shall contact the director to notify the director of<br>the juvenile's presence in the hospital.<br>2. The director, or their designee, must contact the appropriate<br>LME/MCO or prepaid health plan to request an assessment as soon<br>as practicable and, in any event, no later than 24 hours after the<br>determination that the juvenile should not remain at the hospital and<br>no appropriate placement is immediately available.<br>3. The assessment will be performed by either the juvenile's clinical<br>home provider; the hospital, if able and willing; or other qualified<br>licensed clinician within three business days following notification<br>from the director. An assessment pursuant to N.C.G.S §122C-142.2is<br>defined as a comprehensive clinical assessment, psychiatric<br>evaluation, or a substantially equivalent assessment. For purposes of<br>this section, "business days" shall mean Monday through Friday,<br>inclusive of holidays. The hospital shall reasonably cooperate with the<br>LME/MCO or prepaid health plan to provide access to the juvenile<br>during the juvenile's stay in the hospital.<br>4. The LME/MCO or prepaid health plan must, when applicable or<br>required by their contract with the Department, arrange for the<br>assessment.|TheRapid Response Team (RRT) is an NCDHHS cross-divisional<br>team that meets to facilitate the resolution of immediate needs<br>for children in DSS custody who are in need of placement**at the**<br>**identified medically necessary level of care**by removing systemic<br>barriers and facilitating collaboration among county DSS,<br>LME/MCOs, and other child-serving stakeholders. The role of the<br>Rapid Response Team is to assist facilitation for child placement at<br>the appropriate level of care. ## Preparing Parents, Children, and Providers for Placement

|Protocol - What must be done|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|PREPARING PARENTS AND CHILDREN<br>Please refer to in Cross Function Topics in the NC Child Welfare manual.||
|PREPARING PLACEMENT PROVIDERS<br>Please refer to in Cross Function Topics in the NC Child Welfare manual.||

Legal Basis

REASONABLE AND PRUDENT PARENT STANDARD N.C.G.S. § 131D-10.2A states the Reasonable and Prudent Parent Standard is the standard characterized by careful and sensible parental decisions that are reasonably intended to maintain the health, safety, and best interests of the child while at the same time encouraging the emotional and developmental growth of the child that a caregiver shall use when determining whether to allow a child in foster care under the responsibility of the state to participate in extracurricular, enrichment, cultural, and social activities.

In this context, "placement provider" means a foster parent, relative, or non-relative kin with whom a child in the custody or placement responsibility of a county child welfare services agency has been placed, or a designated official for a child care institution in which a child in the custody or placement responsibility of a county child welfare services agency has been placed.

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|The county child welfare services agency must use the Reasonable and<br>Prudent Parent Standard when determining whether to allow a child in<br>county child welfare services agency custody to participate in<br>extracurricular, enrichment, and social activities.<br>•<br>This means activities or items that are generally accepted as<br>suitable for children of the same chronological age or level of<br>maturity or that are determined to be developmentally-<br>appropriate for a child based on the development of cognitive,|Refer to the Reasonable and Prudent Parent Activities Guideand Applying<br>the Reasonable and Prudent Parent Standard Toolfor additional<br>guidance.|

## Out-of-Home Placement Services: Required Contacts

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|REQUIRED CONTACT<br>INITIAL CONTACT WITH CHILD (FOLLOWING PLACEMENT)<br>A face-to-face contact with the child must occur within **7 days**after the day<br>of initial out-of-home placement and within **7 days**after any subsequent<br>placements (any time there is a change in placement).<br>•<br>The visit can take place in the home of the placement provider,<br>at school, or in the community.<br>•<br>Prompt contact following placement provides reassurance to the<br>child and the placement provider. It also allows the county child<br>welfare worker to answer any early questions about the care of the<br>child.<br>•<br>This contact with child(ren) is in addition to any contact or<br>interaction with child(ren) on the day of placement.<br>Documentation in the electronic NC FAST record must reflect diligent efforts<br>made and/or rationale for the delay must be provided if the** 7-day**contact<br>does not occur.<br>INITIAL CONTACT WITH PARENT(S)/CARETAKER(S) (FOLLOWING PLACEMENT)<br>Face to Face contact must be made with all parents or caretakers within 7<br>days of initial placement.|CONTACT WITH CHILD<br>Out-of-home placement is one of the most traumatic events that can<br>occur in a child's life. A timely check regarding the adjustment,<br>answering questions the child may have, and addressing any concerns<br>is trauma informed. Making face-to-face contact with the child within 3<br>days after the day of initial out-of-home placement and any<br>subsequent placements helps to ensure the safety and well-being of<br>the child.<br>During the early stages of placement, the child should have close<br>contact with the county child welfare worker, until a relationship with<br>the placement provider is established. Children removed from their<br>homes need special attention and frequent contacts at the time of<br>placement in county child welfare services agency custody, as well as<br>any time a move from one placement to another is made.<br>The placement provider and the child should know as early as possible<br>when the next face-to-face contact with the county child welfare<br>worker will be, and when the first visit for the child with the birth<br>parents and/or siblings will be.<br>CONTACT WITH PARENT(S)/CARETAKER(S)<br>The 7-day contact with the parents should be made face-to-face at the<br>initial parent/child visitation or 7-day court hearing. The parents should<br>be informed of how the child(ren) is adjusting to the placement, and of<br>any upcoming medical and/or educational appointments.|

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|

|`o` This includes but is not limited to family members who reside<br>outside of the home, relative or non-relative kin, guardians ad<br>litem, service providers, school staff, or others who have<br>information regarding the family.<br>•<br>The agency must have more frequent contact with collaterals when<br>indicated by the child's needs.<br>•<br>Diligent efforts made and/or rationale for the delay must be<br>documented if the minimum requirements for ongoing contacts are<br>not met.<br>OTHER REQUIRED CONTACTS WHEN REUNIFICATION IS THE PRIMARY PLAN<br>When reunification is the primary plan, county child welfare agencies must<br>contact all adult household members residing in the home to which the<br>child will return if reunification is achieved, at least 30 days prior to the<br>child's return home. Background checks on all adult household members<br>must also be conducted prior to recommending the child's return home.<br>NOTE: Background checks on all adult household members must be<br>conducted within 3 days of the child being placed in the home of the<br>parent/caretaker when the court orders the child's return home prior to the|Although important, contact with family members who reside outside<br>the home, relative or non-relative kin or others who have information<br>about the family are not considered persons who are significant to the<br>case.<br>One of the goals of permanency planning is to meet the needs of the<br>child while in foster care, the purpose of collateral contacts is to assess<br>if the needs of the child are being met. Therefore, there should be an<br>effort to speak to a variety of collaterals from month to month over the<br>life of the case. This contact is to gather additional information through<br>multiple perspectives.<br>Service collaboration is a vital part of providing comprehensive,<br>coordinated and family-centered services to families to address child<br>safety and risk. This may be especially valuable on cases that involve<br>domestic violence.<br>OTHER REQUIRED CONTACTS WHEN REUNIFICATION IS THE PRIMARY PLAN<br>Household members include, but are not limited to, relatives, romantic<br>partners, family friends, roommates, and any other adult residing in<br>the home.|

|WATER SAFETY HAZARDS<br>INDIVIDUAL WATER SAFETY PLAN & LICENSED FOSTER HOMES<br>According to Administrative Code 10A NCAC 70E .1112, exterior spaces<br>around the foster home must be clear of any direct access to water.<br>Where there is a known potential water safety hazard, the licensing<br>worker must provide the Individual Water Safety Plan (DSS-5018a) andit<br>must be:<br>•<br>Completed by the foster parent(s)/placement provider(s) within 3<br>calendar days after the day of placement and provide it at the<br>initial visit;<br>•<br>Maintained in the home of the foster parent(s)/placement<br>provider(s) where it is always accessible to all caregivers; and<br>•<br>Updated when changes occur.<br>•<br>Scanned and attached in the electronic record in NC FAST<br>In addition, all licensed foster homes must follow the protocol found in the<br>Foster Home Licensing in the NC Child Welfare manualregarding Exterior<br>Setting and Safety and supervise the child's access to potential safety<br>hazards in accordance with the Individual Water Safety Plan and the prudent<br>parent standard.<br>The county child welfare worker must review the completed Individual<br>Water Hazard Safety Plan with the placement provider within 7 calendar<br>days after the day of placement in the home. Any modifications to the plan<br>based on the individual needs of the child must be made at that time.|Placement providers, whether licensed or unlicensed, should take<br>measures to protect children placed in their home from having<br>unsupervised access to water and other safety hazards.<br>County child welfare workers and supervising agency licensing<br>workers should work closely together to ensure children who are<br>placed in licensed homes do not have access to a water hazards.<br>The Individual Water Safety Plan must be appropriate for the<br>individual needs of the child.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

## Out-of-Home Placement Services: Ongoing Placement Services

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|The first 30 days of permanency planning are critical in the child's transition and to the family's engagement in the change process. Assessment and<br>planning are the activities that lay the foundation for the future work with the family and are important tasks in the initial placement process.<br>Assessment and planning should involve collaborative efforts by all the parties involved with the family. The county child welfare worker should<br>obtain as much information about the child and family as soon as possible so appropriate resources may be chosen and the county child welfare<br>worker can help the placement provider meet the child's needs. The placement provider should be provided any known information to help better<br>care for children, including health and behavioral health history, disability, exposure to trauma, and educational needs.|The first 30 days of permanency planning are critical in the child's transition and to the family's engagement in the change process. Assessment and<br>planning are the activities that lay the foundation for the future work with the family and are important tasks in the initial placement process.<br>Assessment and planning should involve collaborative efforts by all the parties involved with the family. The county child welfare worker should<br>obtain as much information about the child and family as soon as possible so appropriate resources may be chosen and the county child welfare<br>worker can help the placement provider meet the child's needs. The placement provider should be provided any known information to help better<br>care for children, including health and behavioral health history, disability, exposure to trauma, and educational needs.|
|REQUIRED SERVICES FOR CHILDREN<br>For children who are placed in out-of-home placements, county child welfare<br>agencies must provide the following services:<br>•<br>Evaluate and supervise the placement of children to ensure they are<br>receiving proper care;<br>•<br>Face-to-face visit within 7 days of any placement change;<br>•<br>Make, at a minimum, monthly visits with children in foster care<br>placement;<br>•<br>Ensure visitation occurs for children and parents to visit each other;<br>•<br>Ensure visitation occurs for siblings to visit each other, if placed in<br>separate placements;<br>•<br>Ensure the child's well-being needs are met, including emotional and<br>developmental needs;<br>•<br>Engage children in the planning process of the case, including<br>participation in CFT meetings, PPR meetings, and developing the<br>Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement;<br>•<br>Assist children in mitigating the feelings of grief and loss that result<br>from removal from the home;<br>•<br>Ensure children receive all needed evaluations, educational services,<br>medical care and psychological treatment services through referral<br>to other agencies and providers;|Special attention should be given to changes affecting the relationship<br>between the worker and the child/youth, such as changes in<br>frequency of contact, vacation, transfer, or termination of contact<br>through the worker leaving the agency. Changes of the county child<br>welfare worker may bring up the child or youth's fears of separation<br>and abandonment. These fears can lead to disturbances in behavior<br>and subsequent consequences in their relationships with placement<br>providers, teachers, friends, and others.<br>Children need and deserve adequate preparation for changes in<br>service delivery and need to understand why changes are taking place.<br>The child's participation in team planning can help reduce the anxiety<br>both the child and family experiences when individual county child<br>welfare workers leave the agency.<br>Children should be given an opportunity to discuss openly their<br>feelings about a placement. This exchange should occur prior to a<br>placement and during the placement. Often the county child welfare<br>worker is the only person the child feels they can talk to.|

|•<br>Encourage participation in activities that are age- and<br>developmentally-appropriate;<br>•<br>Youth 12 and older must be provided a copy of the Understanding<br>Foster Care- A Handbook for Youth(DSS-1516);<br>•<br>Youth 14 and older must have a credit report run annually, from<br>each of the three credit reporting bureaus (Equifax, Transunion, and<br>Experian);<br>•<br>Develop a TLP with youth ages 14 and older and provide services to<br>help them transition to successful adulthood;<br>•<br>Assess youth ages 16 and 17 to determine their needs for services to<br>prepare them for making the transition from foster care to<br>independent living;<br>•<br>Explain the child's rights while in foster care placement; and<br>•<br>Create and maintain a life book for each child in foster care.<br>_INVOLVEMENT WITH THE DEPARTMENT OF JUVENILE JUSTICE_<br>JUVENILE CONSULTATION MEETINGS<br>According toN.C.G.S 7B-2710, "the parent, guardian, or custodian of a<br>juvenile being provided services through a juvenile consultation shall attend<br>all scheduled meetings with the juvenile court counselor provided sufficient<br>notice of the meeting was given to the parent, guardian, or custodian."<br>If the local county agency has non-secure custody of the juvenile, they must<br>attend all scheduled consultation meetings with the juvenile court counselor<br>and juvenile unless sufficient notice was not given.|Children under age 12 should be provided a copy of the Understanding<br>Foster Care - A Handbook for Youth (DSS-1516), if developmentally-<br>appropriate.<br>The county agency with non-secure custody of the juvenile should be<br>notified about the juvenile consultation meeting through telephone,<br>email, or mail by the Department of Juvenile Justice. The placement<br>provider should attend the consultation meeting with the juvenile,<br>court counselor, and county agency representative.|

|CARE REVIEW TEAM WITH THE DEPARTMENT OF JUVENILE JUSTICE<br>N.C.G.S. 7B-2502(a2) states that "in the case of a juvenile with a suspected<br>mental illness, developmental disability, or intellectual disability that has<br>been adjudicated delinquent, the court shall order that the Juvenile Justice<br>Section of the Division of Adult Correction and Juvenile Justice of the<br>Department of Public Safety make a referral for a comprehensive clinical<br>assessment or equivalent mental health assessment, unless the court finds a<br>comprehensive clinical assessment or equivalent mental health assessment<br>has been conducted within the last 45 days before the adjudication hearing.<br>An assessment ordered by a court under this subsection shall evaluate the<br>developmental, emotional, behavioral, and mental health needs of the<br>juvenile."<br>N.C.G.S. 7B-2502(a3) states that" if an assessment is ordered by the court<br>under subsection (a2) of this section, the court shall review the assessment<br>prior to the date of disposition in the case. If the court finds sufficient<br>evidence that the juvenile has severe emotional disturbance, as defined in<br>N.C.G.S. 7B-1501(24a), or a developmental disability, as defined in N.C.G.S.<br>122C-3(12a), or intellectual disability, as defined in N.C.G.S. 122C-3(17a),<br>that, in the court's discretion, substantially contributed to the juvenile's<br>delinquent behavior, and the juvenile is eligible for a Juvenile Justice Level 3<br>disposition and/or is recommended for a Psychiatric Residential Treatment<br>Facility (PRTF) placement, the court shall order a care review team to be<br>convened by the Juvenile Justice Section of the Division of Adult Correction<br>and Juvenile Justice of the Department of Public Safety and assigned to the<br>case."<br>N.C.G.S. 7B-2502(a4) states that "if a care review team is assigned to a case<br>by the court under subsection (a3) of this section, the care review team shall<br>develop a recommendation plan for appropriate services and resources that<br>address the identified needs of the juvenile. The care review team shall<br>submit a recommendation to the court within 30 calendar days of the date<br>of the court order convening the care review team. The court shall review||

# Out-of-Home Placement Services: Ongoing Placement Services

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|LIFE BOOKS<br>County child welfare agencies must create and maintain a life book for each<br>child in an out-of-home placement.<br>The child's life book must be initiated within 30 days of the child entering<br>custody and updated on an ongoing basis.<br>The following are items that must be in each child's life book:<br>•<br>A copy of a certified birth certificate (or date and time of birth,<br>location of birth, and weight and length at birth);<br>•<br>Schools/daycare centers the child has attended;<br>•<br>Medical information, including immunizations, diseases, and<br>allergies;<br>•<br>Medical history of birth family;<br>•<br>Pictures of the child at various ages;<br>•<br>Names and pictures of siblings; and<br>•<br>Pictures of birth parents.<br>If the items above are not available for the child's life book, documentation<br>in the NC FAST electronic record must reflect efforts made to obtain the<br>items.|LIFE BOOKS<br>Life books should be updated at least every 90 days, and whenever a<br>significant event occurs, including but not limited to a change in<br>placement or school, graduation, birth of a sibling, and changes to the<br>child's medical history.<br>A life book is a tool and a process. It can be applied as a therapeutic<br>tool by assisting the child to cope with emotions that result from the<br>child's experiences. It can also be beneficial for the child's therapist to<br>assist in processing exposure to traumatic events. The collecting,<br>recording, and processing of the child's feelings enlightens the "how"<br>and "why" of what has happened. Life books should be used by county<br>child welfare workers as a tool to assist the child to grieve over losses,<br>celebrate successes, begin to heal in preparation for building trust and<br>attachments in foster care, and to prepare for reunification or<br>adoptive placement if reunification is not possible.<br>Building the life book is a process that involves the continuous<br>collaborative efforts of the child, county child welfare worker, birth<br>parent(s) and foster/adoptive parents. It allows everyone to focus on<br>the child's current, historical, and memorable events and their effects<br>on the child's life.<br>Creating a life book is a process that continues for the duration of the<br>child's journey to permanency. The life book is one page, one picture,<br>and one story at a time. It can start in the present moment and<br>proceed forward with future events. Early in the work with the child<br>and family, the county child welfare worker should begin building the<br>child's life book by taking and procuring photographs of the child,<br>birth family, and foster family. Gradually, prior history should be|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Parents need to know that the goal is to achieve<br>permanency for their child within one year and that it is in their<br>control to determine whether their child's permanent home will be<br>with them or in another permanent placement.<br>The family should be informed of the CFT process to develop the<br>Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement. ## Out-of-Home Placement Services: Ongoing Placement Services

EDUCATIONAL STABILITY

Policy

SCHOOL ATTENDANCE County child welfare agencies must ensure every child in foster care, who has attained the minimum age for compulsory school attendance under state law, is enrolled as a full-time elementary or secondary school student or has completed secondary school.

"Elementary or secondary school student" is defined to include a child that is:

• Enrolled in an institution which provides elementary or secondary education in compliance with state law; • Instructed in elementary or secondary education at home in accordance with state law on home schools; • Participating in an independent study program in elementary or secondary education that is administered by the local school or school district and is in accordance with state law; or • Incapable of attending school on a full-time basis due to the medical condition of the child, which incapability is supported by regularly updated information in the child's case plan.

EVERY STUDENT SUCCEEDS ACT Legal Basis

The Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) (P.L.114-95) reauthorized the 1965 Elementary and Secondary Education Act (ESEA) and includes new provisions that promote educational stability for children in foster care.

Effective December 10, 2016, these provisions complement those in the Fostering Connections Act and require state educational agencies and local educational agencies to work with child welfare agencies to ensure the educational stability of children in foster care. In particular 42 U.S.C. 671 (a)(30) and 42 U.S.C. 675(1)(G) require both county child welfare agencies and local education agencies to collaborate to ensure school changes are minimized, and children in care who do change schools are promptly enrolled. ESSA also removed "awaiting foster care placement" from the McKinney Vento Act definition of homeless.

ESSA requires assurances that children enroll or remain in their school of origin unless a determination is made that it is not in their best interest. That determination must be based on all factors relating to the child's best interest, including consideration of the appropriateness of the current educational setting, and the proximity to the school in which the child is enrolled at the time of the foster care placement. For child welfare county agencies, this requires:

# Out-of-Home Placement Services: Ongoing Placement Services

N.C.G.S. §7B-903.1 states that when a child is in the custody of the county child welfare services agency, the county director may arrange for, provide, or consent to decisions about matters that are generally made by a juvenile's custodian, including but not limited to educational decisions and consenting to the sharing of the juvenile's educational information, without obtaining parental consent.

County child welfare agencies are entitled to all educational records of a child in non-secure custody through the Uninterrupted Scholars Act (P.L. 112- 278).

Each child's privacy and confidentiality are ensured by the federal Educational Records Privacy Act (FERPA) (20 U.S.C § 1232g; 34 CFR Part 99) and the confidentiality of information provisions in the Individuals with Disabilities Education Act (IDEA) (20 U.S.C. §§ 1400-1444).

The following decisions impact educational stability:<br>`o` The decision regarding the child's placement; and<br>`o` The Best Interest Determination (BID) regarding the school the child will attend.<br>If the BID did not occur during the CFT, the child must remain in their current school until the BID is<br>held.<br>The county child welfare agency and local education agency should consider all factors relating to<br>the child's best interest, including:<br>•<br>Preferences of the child;<br>•<br>Preferences of the child's parent(s) or education decision maker(s);<br>•<br>The child's attachment to the school, including meaningful relationships with staff and peers;<br>•<br>Placement of the child's sibling(s);<br>•<br>Influence of the school climate on the child, including safety;<br>•<br>The availability and quality of the services in the school to meet the child's educational and<br>socioemotional needs;<br>•<br>History of school transfers and how they have impacted the child;<br>•<br>How the length of the commute would impact the child, based on the child's developmental<br>stage;<br>•<br>Whether the child is a student with a disability under the IDEA who is receiving special<br>education and related services or a student with a disability under Section 504 who is<br>receiving special education or related aids and services and, if so, the availability of those<br>required services in a school other than the school of origin; and<br>•<br>Whether the child is an EL and is receiving language services, and, if so, the availability of<br>those required services in a school other than the school of origin, consistent with Title VI<br>and the EEOA.|Scheduling of the BID meeting is the<br>responsibility of the county child welfare<br>worker. A change<br>in school placement should only occur before<br>the BID meeting when it is detrimental to the<br>child's best interests to remain in his or her<br>school of origin and requires approval by the<br>county child welfare agency director (or<br>designee).<br>One of the responsibilities of the county child<br>welfare services agency point of contact is<br>data collection regarding compliance with<br>educational stability policy for all children in a<br>county child welfare services agency's<br>custody.|
