## Out-of-Home Placement Services: Ongoing Placement Services

PARENT/CHILD VISITATION/FAMILY TIME

Legal Basis N.C.G.S. 7B-905.1 states an order that removes custody of a juvenile from a parent, guardian, or custodian or that continues the juvenile's placement outside the home shall provide for visitation that is in the best interests of the juvenile consistent with the juvenile's health and safety, including no visitation. The court may specify in the order conditions under which visitation may be suspended. If the juvenile is placed or continued in the custody or placement responsibility of a county department of social services, the court may order the director to arrange, facilitate, and supervise a visitation plan expressly approved or ordered by the court. The plan shall indicate the minimum frequency and length of visits and whether the visits shall be supervised. Unless the court orders otherwise, the director shall have discretion to determine who will supervise visits when supervision is required, to determine the location of visits, and to change the day and time of visits in response to scheduling conflicts, illness of the child or party, or extraordinary circumstances. A parent's positive result from a drug screen alone is insufficient to deny the parent court-ordered visitation with the juvenile. The director shall promptly communicate a limited and temporary change in the visitation schedule to the affected party. Any ongoing change in the visitation schedule shall be communicated to the party in writing and state the reason for the change. For parents with unsupervised visitation that have a positive result from a drug screen, the department of social services shall expeditiously file a motion for review and request that a hearing be scheduled within 30 days for the court to review the visitation plan to ensure the safety of the child. While the motion is pending, the director may temporarily impose supervision requirements to all or part of the visitation plan. The director shall promptly communicate the limited and temporary change in the visitation plan to the affected party. No motion or notice of hearing is required if a review or permanency planning hearing is already scheduled to be heard within 30 days of the imposed suspension requirement.

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|INITIAL PARENT/CHILD FAMILY TIME<br>Family Time (visitation) must occur between the parent(s)/<br>caretaker(s) and the child(ren) within 7 calendar days of the child<br>entering agency custody.<br>Family Time (visitation) between siblings (if placed in separate<br>placements) must occur within 7 calendar days of entering agency<br>custody.|The initial visit between the parent and child should be as structured as<br>possible. In many cases, the Family Time and Contact (Visitation) Plan will not<br>be developed prior to the initial visit. It is important to prepare the<br>parent(s)/caretaker(s) for the visit by discussing expectations, who will<br>participate, and what is and is not appropriate for the visit.<br>Family Time and Contact Plans should be addressed in every court hearing and<br>reflected in every court order, particularly when unsupervised visits are<br>considered.|

# Out-of-Home Placement Services: Ongoing Placement Services

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|DEVELOPING THE FAMILY TIME AND CONTACT (VISITATION) PLAN<br>The Family Time and Contact (Visitation) Plan(DSS-5242),<br>generated in NC FAST, must:<br>•<br>State, for each child, the type, level of supervision,<br>frequency, duration, and location of visits;<br>•<br>Always be current;<br>•<br>Be revised as often as necessary; and<br>•<br>Be signed by all parties.<br>Each must address the following critical elements:<br>•<br>Attendees/participants approved by the county child<br>welfare services agency to attend the visits;<br>•<br>Transportation arrangements for the parents and the child;<br>•<br>Whether visits will be supervised or monitored, and by<br>whom; and<br>•<br>Whether other kinds of contacts are appropriate such as<br>telephone calls, emails or letters, skype, or social media,<br>and if monitoring of them is needed.<br>NOTE: The Family Time and Contact Plan must be signed by the<br>parents. If the parents refuse to sign, the county child welfare<br>worker must document their refusal on the form, scan and<br>attached it to the electronic record in NC FAST.<br>Separate Family Time and Contact Plans must be developed for<br>each parent, if they are not visiting the child together, and for<br>siblings, if placed separately.|If parent attorneys advise parents not to sign anything, involve the attorney in<br>developing the Family Time and Contact Plan.<br>Parents have the responsibility for and maintain their right for visitation with<br>their children.<br>Frequent and meaningful visitation between parent/child should occur because:<br>•<br>Visits maintain and improve the parent/child relationship;<br>•<br>Visits enable children to see their parents realistically and rationally and<br>can help calm separation fears;<br>•<br>Visitation is often the only means of maintaining, improving, or<br>developing the child's relationship with their parents;<br>•<br>Visits provide the opportunity for parents to improve their parenting<br>skills and to demonstrate their ability to care for their child;<br>•<br>Visits provide the county child welfare worker the opportunity to<br>observe and to evaluate the parent-child relationship;<br>•<br>Visits are a good indicator of the possibility of reunification; and<br>•<br>Visits provide documentation of the parent's progress.<br>Visits can be a motivator for parents who are making progress on the objectives<br>of their Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement. When county child<br>welfare workers have observed a parent's progress, they can ask the court to<br>review the Family Time and Contact Plan and revise it to allow more frequent<br>visits, longer visits, or unsupervised visits, as appropriate.<br>Children need visits to:<br>•<br>Keep a connection to their family;<br>•<br>Mitigate their grief;<br>•<br>Have their worth reaffirmed;<br>•<br>Have the assurance that their parents "exist"; and<br>•<br>Re-establish and strengthen a relationship with their parents.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 118 -->
## Transportation of High-Risk Juveniles

Legal Basis Transportation of High-Risk Juveniles:

Section 9J.13 of the 2023 Appropriations Act, S.L. 2023-134, enacts G.S. 7B-905.2, which addresses the transportation of juveniles in DSS custody with serious emotional mental, or behavioral disturbances that pose a risk of harm to self or others and who reside outside of a residential placement because of those disturbances. These juveniles are defined as "high-risk juveniles" at G.S. 7B-905.2(a)(1).

DSS may make a written request to or enter into a transportation agreement with a "high-risk juvenile transporter" to transport the juvenile. A highrisk juvenile transporter is a law enforcement agency, the Division of Juvenile Justice of the Department of Public Safety (DJJ), or the Department of Adult Corrections (DAC). G.S. 7B-905.2(a)(2).

If the high-risk juvenile transporter agrees, transportation must be provided in the county where the juvenile resides but is not limited to that county. The cost and expenses of transportation are the responsibility of the county DSS with custody of the high-risk juvenile. The high-risk juvenile transporter may use reasonable force and reasonable restraints (determined by the high-risk juvenile transporter) to restrain the high-risk juvenile if necessary to protect the transporter or others.

The high-risk juvenile transporter is immune from criminal or civil liability when taking reasonable measures under this statute so long as the transporter was acting in good faith and did not engage in gross negligence, wanton conduct, or intentional wrongdoing.

This law is retroactive to July 1, 2023.

## Shared Parenting

| Legal Basis | |
|---|---|
|North Carolina Administrative Code70 E .1104 requires that foster parents shall develop partnerships with children and their parents or guardians,<br>help children maintain and develop relationships that will keep them connected to their pasts, and help children placed in the home build a positive<br>self-concept and positive family, cultural, and racial identity.|North Carolina Administrative Code70 E .1104 requires that foster parents shall develop partnerships with children and their parents or guardians,<br>help children maintain and develop relationships that will keep them connected to their pasts, and help children placed in the home build a positive<br>self-concept and positive family, cultural, and racial identity.|
|Policy|Policy|
|Foster parents must engage in shared parenting by:<br>•<br>Developing partnerships with children and their parents or guardians;<br>•<br>Helping children maintain and develop relationships that will keep them connected to their pasts; and<br>•<br>Helping children placed out of their own home build a positive self-identity and positive family, cultural, and racial identity.|Foster parents must engage in shared parenting by:<br>•<br>Developing partnerships with children and their parents or guardians;<br>•<br>Helping children maintain and develop relationships that will keep them connected to their pasts; and<br>•<br>Helping children placed out of their own home build a positive self-identity and positive family, cultural, and racial identity.|
|Protocol - Whatyou must do|Guidance - Howyou should do it|
|No later than 14 days of a child being placed out of the home by<br>a county child welfare services agency, a shared parenting<br>meeting between the parent(s) and the placement provider<br>must occur.<br>This includes placement in:<br>•<br>Licensed foster homes (therapeutic and family foster<br>care);<br>•<br>Relative and non-relative kinship placements;<br>•<br>Group home placements; and<br>•<br>Any other placement in which the county child welfare<br>services agency has legal custody of a child and the child<br>is separated from their parent or caretaker.<br>Foster parents, whether supervised by a private agency or a<br>county child welfare services agency, must participate in shared<br>parenting.|Trauma-Informed Partnering for Safety and Permanence Model Approach to<br>Partnerships in Parenting- (TIPS-MAPP) training is a 10-week training curriculum<br>that is used as a tool in the mutual assessment of prospective foster and adoptive<br>parents in their ability to successfully provide trauma-informed care for children in<br>county child welfare services agency custody.<br>Shared parenting and shared parenting meetings can be an intimidating process<br>for both foster and birth parents. Much like Child and Family Team meetings,<br>preparation is the key for shared parenting to succeed.<br>This requires advanced planning by county child welfare workers, so all parties<br>understand the purpose of the meeting is to discuss the care of the child, not "the<br>case." The meeting is not to assign blame. It is first and foremost about creating<br>the best possible transition for the child. Sharing parental responsibilities can be<br>enjoyable activities such as working on the child's life book together, exchanging<br>pictures, reading with the child, etc. They can also plan a joint fun activity that is<br>specifically catered to the child.|

# Shared Parenting

|Before the shared parenting meeting, the county child welfare<br>worker must:<br>• Be aware that they do not impose their own biases<br>about a birth parent's previous decisions;<br>• Model positive communication about the birth parent to<br>the foster parent and about the foster parent to the<br>birth parent;<br>• Brief the foster parents about any birth parent fears or<br>needs and help the foster parents understand these<br>needs;<br>• Be prepared to discuss how the needs and interests of the<br>child will be recognized during the shared parenting<br>meeting;<br>• Plan for the birth parent visiting with the child, if<br>appropriate;<br>• Make visitation arrangements when the child is visiting<br>with the birth parent before or after the meeting; and<br>• Encourage the foster parents to share with the birth<br>parents a positive attribute they see in the child.<br>The county child welfare worker must prepare the foster parents<br>and birth parents to exchange information essential to shared<br>parenting, including:<br>• Medical information;<br>• School progress;<br>• Sleeping habits;<br>• Eating habits;<br>• Response to discipline; and<br>• Any of the children's strengths and needs.|Deciding when and where the shared parenting meeting should be held, the<br>county child welfare worker should:<br>• Take into consideration the work schedules of the foster and birth parents<br>as well as children's schedules, especially if there is a need for child care<br>while the adults discuss parenting issues; and<br>• Ask the foster and birth parents where they would like to meet. Meeting in<br>a neutral location that allows for privacy is important. The following can be<br>good options:<br>• A neighborhood recreation center or social center;<br>• The library; or<br>• A child's therapist office.<br>County child welfare workers should initially focus on items that might seem<br>simple but can be very important such as:<br>• Asking for a picture of the family to have for the child; and<br>• Discussing the child's favorite foods, toys, clothes, activities, likes, and<br>dislikes.<br>Both the birth and foster parents should be encouraged to talk openly. County child<br>welfare workers should assist foster and birth parents in managing conflict by:<br>• Recognizing the fears of all parties;<br>• Focusing on the strengths;<br>• Looking beyond behaviors to identify needs; and<br>• Developing interventions to meet needs.<br>County child welfare workers should also assist foster and birth parents in<br>understanding cultural or family differences by helping them to:<br>• Cultivate a mutual understanding and appreciation of religious beliefs and<br>practices;|

# Shared Parenting

|INVOLVING RELATIVES<br>County child welfare agencies must decide whether to include<br>relatives in shared parenting meetings by considering the<br>following:<br>• The relationship between the birth parent and the relative;<br>• Will the relative disrupt the development of the<br>partnership between the birth parent and foster parent?<br>• Does the relative have information critical to the daily<br>care of the child, and is this information needed to meet<br>the needs of the child and/or provide support to the<br>biological parents?<br>• Would involving the relative disrupt the long-term goals<br>between the biological and foster parent?<br>• Could the relative be a mentor and support that would<br>help develop a continued partnership beyond<br>reunification?<br>• Other opportunities for the relative to be a part of the<br>child's life/planning, such as involvement in child and<br>family team meetings; and<br>• Will the decision alienate the birth parent or relative?<br>(Make sure all feel heard.)|• Consider facilitating a phone conference call or web meeting to begin<br>developing a relationship between the birth parent and placement<br>provider.<br>Prior to removal, the parents may have had tremendous support from other<br>relatives in raising their child. For example, a grandmother that has been the<br>primary caretaker for the child over the past 6 months may have some information<br>that is essential for the care of the child. The biological parents may want the<br>relative provider to be a part of a shared parenting meeting.<br>Eventually, birth parents and placement providers may come to build a good<br>relationship and choose to share personal information with each other.<br>SAFETY DURING SHARED PARENTING<br>The safety of the participants should always be considered when planning shared<br>parenting meetings. The county child welfare worker should be aware if there has<br>been a history of domestic violence between birth parents as well as if a birth<br>parent has any history of violence towards others.<br>It would not be appropriate to facilitate or encourage any shared parenting<br>meetings together if there are any court orders, including those imposed by<br>probation and parole, that do not allow contact between the birth parents.<br>However, it is possible that separate meetings could take place with the placement<br>providers and each individual birth parent at separate times. Document any safety<br>concerns.|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Shared Parenting

|CONFIDENTIALITY<br>It is recognized that placement providers have a need to know<br>medical conditions that a child may have. Re-disclosure of the<br>information is prohibited without consent of the child, parent, or<br>guardian.<br>County child welfare workers must:<br>• Avoid sharing information about the birth parents to the<br>placement providers or about placement providers to<br>the birth parents if it is not information that is pertinent<br>to the child's care.<br>• Inform birth parents and placement providers that<br>information shared within a shared parenting meeting<br>remains confidential.<br>• Discuss any questions with their supervisors and seek<br>out agency policy around specific situations for the<br>sharing of information.|Consider what special arrangements can be made to help everyone feel safe and<br>comfortable such as:<br>• Choose a safe location;<br>• Create specific ground rules and expectations ahead of time together with<br>all participants that are catered to the specific needs;<br>• If the meeting cannot be held safely, do not hold the meeting; and<br>• Choose other avenues such as a phone conference call to facilitate the<br>meeting.|
|---|---|

## Permanency Planning

|Policy|Legal Basis|
|---|---|
|The county child welfare services agency must be committed to a<br>permanent resolution of the child's foster care status. Permanent<br>resolutions include:<br>•<br>Reunification;<br>•<br>Guardianship;<br>•<br>Custody;<br>•<br>Another Planned Permanent Living Arrangement (APPLA);<br>•<br>Reinstatement of Parental Rights (RPR); or<br>•<br>Adoption.<br>Permanency planning promotes a permanent living situation:<br>•<br>For every child entering the foster care system;<br>•<br>With an adult with whom the child has a continuous, reciprocal<br>relationship; and<br>•<br>Within a minimum amount of time.<br>For children and youth in the custody and placement responsibility of<br>the county child welfare services agency, permanence occurs when they<br>have a lasting, nurturing, legally secure relationship with at least one<br>adult that is characterized by mutual commitment.<br>A "legally secure placement" is defined as a placement in which the<br>direct caretaker has the legal authority to make parental decisions on<br>behalf of the child. Permanency planning are all the casework activities<br>and decision-making activities that guide permanency planning. They are<br>the following:<br>•<br>When children must be removed from their parents,<br>reunification should occur as soon as possible when concerns<br>that precipitated the child's removal have been alleviated, and|N.C.G.S. § 7B-906.2 (b)states at any permanency planning hearing, the<br>court shall adopt concurrent permanent plans and shall identify the<br>primary plan and secondary plan. Reunification shall remain a primary or<br>secondary plan unless the court relieves the department of making<br>reunification efforts at the initial disposition under G.S. 7B-901(c),<br>previously made written findings under G.S. 7B-906.1(d)(3) or makes<br>written findings that reunification efforts clearly would be unsuccessful<br>or would be inconsistent with the juvenile's health or safety. When the<br>court makes written findings that reunification has been eliminated as a<br>permanent plan (primary or secondary), concurrent planning is not<br>required (H612 Section 1.13(b) amending N.C.G.S. § 7B-906.2(b)). The<br>court shall order the county department of social services to make<br>efforts toward finalizing the primary and concurrent permanent plans<br>and may specify efforts that are reasonable to timely achieve<br>permanence for the juvenile.<br>PL 113-183requires agencies to provide documentation at each<br>permanency hearing of the "intensive, ongoing, and, as of the date of the<br>hearing, unsuccessful efforts made by the county child welfare services<br>agency to return the child or secure a placement with a fit and willing<br>relative (including adult siblings), a legal guardian, or an adoptive parent,<br>including through efforts that utilize search technology (including social<br>media) to find biological family members for children."<br>According toN.C.G.S. § 7B-101 (18), reasonable efforts are defined as the<br>diligent use of preventive or reunification services by a department of<br>social services when a juvenile's remaining at home or returning home is<br>consistent with achieving a safe, permanent home for the juvenile within a<br>reasonable period of time. If a court of competent jurisdiction determines<br>that thejuvenile is not to be returned home, then reasonable efforts|

The<br>following timeframes must be met for the corresponding<br>permanent plans:<br>`o` Reunification: 12 months<br>`o` Guardianship/Custody: 18 months<br>`o` Adoption 24 months|means the diligent and timely use of Permanency Planning Services by a<br>department of social services to develop and implement a permanent plan<br>for the juvenile.<br>According to N.C.G.S. § 7B-101 (18b), reunification is defined as placement<br>of the juvenile in the home of either parent or placement of the juvenile in<br>the home of a guardian or custodian from whose home the child was<br>removed by court order.<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-903.1(c) states if a juvenile is removed from the home and<br>placed in the custody or placement responsibility of a county department<br>of social services, the director shall not allow unsupervised visitation with<br>or return physical custody of the juvenile to the parent, guardian,<br>custodian, or caretaker without a hearing at which the court finds that the<br>juvenile will receive proper care and supervision in a safe home. NCGS §<br>7B-903.1(c) further states that before a county child welfare services<br>agency may recommend return of physical custody of the juvenile to the<br>parent, guardian, custodian, or caretaker from whom the juvenile was<br>removed, the agency must first observe that parent, guardian, custodian,<br>or caretaker with the juvenile for at least two visits that support a<br>recommendation to return physical custody. Each observation visit shall<br>consist of an observation of not less than one hour with the juvenile, and<br>each observation visit shall be conducted at least seven days apart.<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-600states that guardianship assigns legal authority for the<br>guardian to act on behalf of the child without further county child welfare<br>services agency involvement, but with continued supervision of the court.<br>The authority of the guardian continues until the court terminates the<br>guardianship or until the child is 18 years of age or is emancipated by the<br>court. |Child welfare agencies must never cease efforts to obtain permanency<br>for children and youth in its custody or placement responsibility,<br>regardless of age or behaviors.<br>Both in-state and out-of-state options must be considered when making<br>reasonable efforts to place the child in accordance with the permanency<br>plan and to finalize the permanency plan.|authority cannot be removed unless the guardian is determined by the<br>court to be unfit.N.C.G.S. § 7B-600(c) If the court appoints an individual<br>guardian of the person pursuant to this section, the court shall verify that<br>the person being appointed as guardian of the juvenile understands the<br>legal significance of the appointment and will have adequate resources to<br>care appropriately for the juvenile. The fact that the prospective guardian<br>has provided a stable placement for the juvenile for at least six<br>consecutive months is evidence that the person has adequate resources.<br>N.C.G.S. § 7B-912(c) states if the court finds the juvenile is 16 or 17 years<br>old, the county child welfare services agency has made diligent efforts to<br>place the juvenile, however the court has found compelling reasons exist<br>that it is not in the best interest of the juvenile to be placed permanently<br>with a parent or relative in a guardianship or adoptive placement, and<br>Another Planned Permanent Living Arrangement is the best permanency<br>plan for the juvenile, the court shall approve APPLA, as defined by P.L. 113-<br>183, as the juvenile's primary permanent plan.<br>Reinstatement of Parental Rights (RPR) became a permanency option<br>when N.C.G.S. § 7B-1114 went into effect June 1, 2011. Circumstances that<br>would allow this permanency option are very narrow. The youth, the<br>county child welfare services agency, the parent whose rights have been<br>terminated, or the youth's GAL attorney may file a motion to reinstate<br>parental rights. When the court enters an order to reinstate a former<br>parent's rights, these rights include custody, control, and support of the<br>youth.|

# Permanency Planning

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|REUNIFICATION<br>Reunification must occur as soon as possible when concerns that<br>precipitated the child's removal have been alleviated, and parents can<br>demonstrate their ability to provide a minimum sufficient level of care<br>and ensure safety. To make this assessment county child welfare<br>workers will:<br>•<br>Observe and supervising child or youth and parental/caretaker<br>contact<br>•<br>Planning and preparation activities with the family<br>•<br>Results of decision-making tools<br>Reunification must remain a primary or secondary plan until the court<br>makes written findings that such efforts would be unsuccessful or<br>inconsistent with the juvenile's need for a safe, permanent home within<br>a reasonable period (N.C.G.S. § 7B-906.1(d)(3)). Whether reunification is<br>the primary or secondary plan, efforts to reunify the family must not<br>cease until the above findings have been made by the court. When the<br>court makes written findings that reunification has been eliminated as a<br>permanent plan (primary or secondary), concurrent planning is not<br>required (H612 Section 1.13(b) amending N.C.G.S. § 7B-906.2(b)).<br>Reasonable efforts to reunify the child with the parent(s) or caretaker<br>must be demonstrated and documented to the court.<br>A child who has been removed from the custody of a parent(s) or<br>caretaker must not be returned for any period without a judicial review<br>and findings of fact to show the child will receive proper care and<br>supervision as observed during a trial home visit.|REUNIFICATION<br>Reunification should be considered when:<br>•<br>The issues that precipitated the child's removal have been<br>addressed and resolved; and<br>•<br>Risk to the child has been reduced to a reasonable level; and<br>•<br>The parents have made changes in their behavior and<br>circumstances that were identified as needing to change before<br>the child could be returned safely to the home; and<br>•<br>The parent has demonstrated capacity and willingness to provide<br>appropriate care for the child; and<br>•<br>The child's safety and care in the home is reasonably expected to<br>remain secure; and<br>•<br>Supports from the agency and community are in place to assist the<br>family to remain intact.<br>Family Reunification Services are available to families in which the child<br>has been removed from the home. These services support the family's<br>effort to resolve the conditions which led to the child's removal and to<br>build protective factors that enable the child to return home. For more<br>information regarding these services, please see "Family Reunification<br>Services" in Cross Function Topics in the NC Child Welfare manual.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|GUARDIANSHIP<br>When reunification efforts are determined to be contrary to the health,<br>safety, or best interest of a child who is in the legal custody or<br>placement authority of the county child welfare services agency, the<br>county must assess relative or kinship placements as a permanency<br>option, including both maternal and paternal relatives. If the family is<br>willing to provide a permanent home for the child but is not willing to<br>adopt, then legal guardianship must be offered to the family as<br>alternatives. Guardianship must only be considered when reunification<br>and adoption are ruled out as permanency options.<br>The legal authority of the guardian includes:<br>•<br>The care, custody, and control of the juvenile;<br>•<br>The authority to arrange placement for the juvenile;<br>•<br>The right to represent the juvenile in legal actions before the<br>court;|GUARDIANSHIP<br>Guardianship can be awarded to a relative or any other person deemed<br>suitable by the court. Persons other than relatives to consider include<br>foster parents or adults who have a kinship bond with the child, even if<br>they are not related by blood.<br>Juvenile court guardianship does not confer authority over the disposition<br>of a child's estate or management of his assets. If the child has an estate or<br>receives income such as through Social Security Administration (SSA), and<br>there is no representative available, a separate court action should be<br>initiated to establish guardianship of the estate.<br>For a parent to reverse guardianship, the court must find that the parent is<br>willing and able to care for the child, and the guardian is unfit or has<br>neglected his or her duties.|

For<br>KinGAP, the term relative includes not only biological family members but<br>also non-relatives who had a significant relationship with the child(ren) or<br>their caregiver before the child entered foster care. (See the eligibility<br>criteria for more details.)<br>Moreover, to be eligible for KinGAP or GAP, adoption and reunification<br>must no longer be viable options. Guardianship Assistance Program (GAP):<br>• Purpose: Provides financial support to children who<br>leave foster care to live with a legal guardian.<br>• Eligibility --- Until the child is 18 years of age, the child is eligible for<br>guardianship assistance payments if all of the criteria are met:<br>o The child has attained 10 years of age, but has not reached<br>his or her 18th birthday, and demonstrates a strong<br>attachment to the licensed prospective guardian and the<br>prospective guardian has a strong commitment to caring<br>permanently for the child; if age 14, the child must be<br>consulted regarding the guardianship arrangement. NOTE:<br>A child under 10 is NOT eligible for guardianship assistance<br>even if their sibling qualifies.|• Kinship Guardianship Assistance Program (KinGAP)<br>Allows children and youth to maintain their family and community<br>roots when they can no longer live with their parents and adoption<br>is not an appropriate permanent plan. For these purposes, a<br>relative is defined as a person related to the minor child by blood,<br>marriage, adoption, or an individual that has a substantial<br>relationship with the minor child or the minor child's parent prior<br>to the child being placed in foster care. KinGAP funding comes<br>from IV-E (through the foster care episode) or IV-B (State-Funded<br>Foster Home Funds). County CWAs should inform relatives and<br>fictive kin early on of their option to become licensed foster<br>parents, and about available funding for licensed providers. They<br>have the right to know and understand their options as it pertains<br>to the care they provide to children in foster care. If the family is<br>already licensed, it will save time later if the child's plan becomes<br>guardianship and the child is otherwise eligible for guardianship<br>assistance payments.<br>• Successor Guardians (KinGAP only): In the event of the death<br>or incapacity of the legal relative guardian(s), the child is still<br>eligible for assistance if a successor guardian is named in the<br>KinGAP & GAP Agreement (DSS-1810). For relative guardians,<br>naming a successor guardian allows for the continuation of<br>benefits with only a short suspension while the county child<br>welfare agency (CWA) assists the successor guardian in<br>obtaining legal guardianship and completing the necessary<br>paperwork and safety checks. The conversation about<br>successor guardianship should begin as early as possible to<br>allow the legal relative guardian an opportunity to consider<br>options and discuss the implications with the named<br>individual. In the event of the death or incapacity of the legal<br>relative guardian(s), the Successor Guardian named on the<br>original KinGAP & GAP Agreement|

# Permanency Planning

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|

|CUSTODY<br>Legal custody is an acceptable permanency option, although it does not<br>have the same level of security or permanency as adoption or<br>guardianship. Custody can be challenged before the court and<br>terminated any time there is a change in circumstances, regardless of<br>the fitness of the custodian.<br>When the primary or secondary permanency plan is custody, the county<br>child welfare services agency must:<br>•<br>Demonstrate diligent efforts to locate a suitable person who is<br>willing to assume custody of the child.<br>•<br>Provide information to the potential custodian about more<br>permanent and legally secure options, including adoption and<br>legal guardianship.<br>•<br>Assess the suitability of the home for custodial placement and<br>make a recommendation of their findings.<br>•<br>Evaluate and discuss any potential conflicts the custodian may<br>have with the birth parent(s).<br>Efforts to achieve a permanent plan of custody must be documented in<br>the electronic NC FAST record and the court report.<br>If the agency is unsuccessful in locating a person willing to assume<br>custody of the child within one year of the court ordering a primary<br>permanent plan of custody, the permanent plan must be changed unless<br>the agency can justify to the court why the plan should remain<br>"custody." Justification includes the agency's progress toward locating a<br>suitableperson willing to assume legal responsibility for the child.|CUSTODY<br>Legal custody is not well defined in law; however, it typically implies<br>responsibility for the oversight of a child's care, protection, education, and<br>personal relationships. Custody has most of the same advantages and<br>disadvantages as guardianship. The specific rights and responsibilities of a<br>custodian, however, are defined by the court order rather than being fully<br>defined in law. The rights of a custodian can be as extensive as those of a<br>guardian, or more limited.<br>Just as with guardianship, custody can be awarded to a relative or any<br>other person deemed suitable by the court. Foster parents or adults who<br>have a kinship bond with the child, even if they are not related by blood,<br>should be considered as possible custodians.<br>Legal custody does not confer authority over the disposition of a child's<br>estate or management of his assets. If the child has an estate or receives<br>income such as through Social Security Administration (SSA), and there is<br>no such representative available, separate court action should be initiated<br>to establish guardianship of the estate.<br>Prior to recommending custody be awarded to a specific person(s), the<br>county child welfare services agency should assess the potential custodian<br>by completing theComprehensive Provider Assessment(DSS-5204). See<br>also Instructions for Kinship Care Assessments (DSS-5204ins).<br>For more information, see Kinship Care. <br>Legal custody can be reversed if the court finds the parent is willing and<br>able to provide appropriate care for the child.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

A shortage or lack of foster family homes shall not be an<br>acceptable reason for determining that the needs of the child cannot be<br>met in a foster family home (42 U.S.C. § 675a(c)(C)).<br>**Required Review Intervals:**<br>APPLA must be reviewed at least every six months, or more often as<br>needed, at a facilitated Child and Family Team (CFT) meeting, which<br>includes the youth, caregiver and their supports, as well as the agency<br>LINKS Coordinator. |ADOPTION<br>Adoption is the permanent plan offering the most stability to the child<br>who cannot return to their parents. Factors to consider include whether<br>the child is likely to return home and whether the child can be freed for<br>adoption. For the child to be adopted, both parents must voluntarily<br>relinquish their parental rights, or their parental rights must be<br>terminated by the court. Adoption by relatives or kin must be considered<br>if the relative or kin are willing to adopt and canprovide a safe home.|ADOPTION<br>When adoption is being considered as a permanent plan, satisfactory<br>answers to the following questions should be considered:<br>•<br>Have all relative placement options been considered and<br>eliminated?<br>•<br>Have the child's ethnic and cultural needs been considered and<br>addressed?|

|Current foster parents of the child have the right to be considered as<br>prospective adoptive placement after a termination of parental rights<br>hearing or relinquishment pursuant to G.S. 7B-1112.1.<br>When the court has ordered a primary permanent plan of adoption, the<br>county child welfare services agency must:<br>•<br>File a petition for termination of parental rights within 60<br>calendar days of the hearing that determines the primary<br>permanent plan is adoption unless the court makes other<br>findings. Note: there must be legal grounds to terminate each<br>parent's rights.<br>When the child is legally freed for adoption (both parents' rights have<br>been voluntarily relinquished, or terminated by the court), the agency<br>must do the following:<br>•<br>Make every effort to locate and place the child in an appropriate<br>adoptive home;<br>•<br>Develop a child-specific, written strategy for recruitment of an<br>adoptive home within 30 days. At a minimum, the plan must<br>document the child-specific recruitment efforts such as the use<br>of state, regional, and national adoption exchanges, including<br>electronic exchange systems, to facilitate orderly and timely in-<br>state and interstate placements;<br>•<br>Develop a Child Profile that describes the child needing<br>placement to be available for prospective adoptive families;<br>•<br>Conduct or arrange for a Pre-Placement Assessment (PPA) or a<br>PPA Addendum based on potential adoptive family's status; and<br>•<br>Register all children who are free for adoption and who are not<br>in their identified adoptive home with theNorth Carolina|•<br>Has the best interest of the child been considered and<br>documented?<br>•<br>Are the parents willing to relinquish their rights, or is the agency<br>ready to proceed with termination of parental rights?<br>•<br>Do legal grounds for termination of parental rights exist?<br>•<br>Is the child already living with caretakers who are willing to adopt?<br>•<br>How soon can the child be placed in an adoptive home?<br>•<br>How long will the court process take?<br>•<br>Who will help the child through the placement process?<br>•<br>Has a pool of potential adoptive families been recruited, or is the<br>agency willing to commit to child-specific recruitment?<br>•<br>Have the child's specific needs and strengths been thoroughly<br>assessed and evaluated?<br>•<br>Has a placement option that will be able to meet the child's needs<br>been identified?<br>•<br>What is the child's relationship with siblings?<br>•<br>Should the child be placed with siblings and, if so, can this be<br>accomplished?<br>•<br>Is the child able to accept "parenting?"<br>When considering relatives or kin, care should be taken in assessing this<br>option to consider whether there may be conflict or divided loyalties<br>between the parent of the child and the adopting relatives, and how these<br>issues would be handled. If an adoption by relative or kin can be achieved,<br>the child's sense of identity and family history can be preserved.<br>After a termination of parental rights or relinquishment, the county child<br>welfare agency should contact the current placement provider to<br>determine if they want to be considered as the prospective adoptive<br>placement and notify theplacementprovider of the next required steps.|

|**Post-Adoption Mediated Contact Agreement**<br>H612, section 1.1, amending N.C.G.S. 7B-101(16a)provides that prior to<br>executing a relinquishment of a child for adoption, the parent(s) of a<br>minor who is in the custody of the county department of social services<br>(DSS) and the prospective adoptive parent(s) may voluntarily participate<br>in a court-approved mediation program to reach a Post-Adoption<br>Contact Agreement.|been confirmed by the court, a notice must be given to the parent's<br>counsel and the parent must be advised of the right to seek the advice of<br>their counsel prior to executing the relinquishment and to have the<br>parent's counsel present while executing the relinquishment.<br>When adoption by a relative, kin, or foster parent is not an option, the<br>agency should place the child in an approved adoptive home. Recruitment activities should include the use of media resources and<br>the faith community.<br>A child may be placed in a "legal risk" placement before the child is legally<br>cleared for adoption. The purpose of legal risk placement is to move the<br>child into a permanent home as soon as possible without jeopardizing the<br>legal or social well-being of the child. A legal risk placement does not allow<br>the agency to consent to the child's adoption. Therefore, the home in<br>which the child is to be placed must be licensed as a foster home or<br>approved by a court order. Legal risk placements are appropriate when the<br>child is not yet legally free for adoption but there is a high probability that<br>parental rights will be terminated.<br>**Post-Adoption Mediated Contact Agreement**<br>ThePermanency Planning Mediation Program, operated by the NC<br>Administrative Office of the Courts, supports improved adoption<br>outcomes by giving biological parents and prospective adoptive parents<br>the opportunity to voluntarily create a legally recognized post-adoption<br>contact agreement before rights are relinquished. # Permanency Planning

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|

|A Post-Adoption Mediated Contact Agreement is a voluntarily mediated<br>agreement reached by the designated parties, approved by a district<br>court judge and incorporated into a district court order, that allows<br>specifically described post-adoption contact with a child, including<br>visitation, sharing of information, and communication such as the<br>exchange of letters, electronic communication, and telephone contact<br>(H612, section 1.1, amending N.C.G.S. 7B-101(16a)). The Permanency<br>Mediation Program shall be administered by NCAOC Court Programs<br>Manager of the Child Custody and Visitation/Permanency Mediation<br>Program. <br>**Permanency Mediation Process for Post-Adoption Contact**<br>**Agreements:**<br>**1. **The referring judge is responsible for completing the proper<br>NCAOC forms or providing an order informing the mediator of<br>the issues for mediation, the parameters of an agreement, as<br>well as the names and contact information of all the parties<br>ordered/referred to attend.<br>2. Parents and prospective adoptive parents who are referred to<br>mediation by the court to discuss post-adoption contact attend<br>**voluntarily**. Other individuals may be invited to participate in<br>the mediation by mutual consent of the parent(s) executing a<br>relinquishment and the prospective adoptive parent(s). Invited<br>attendees are not parties to any agreement reached during<br>mediation and shall not receive a copy of any agreement. Minor<br>biological parent(s) have legal capacity to participate in<br>mediation and enter post-adoption contact agreements and be<br>bound by the agreement.<br>3. One or two qualified mediators will be assigned by the program<br>manager or designee. The mediator(s) will schedule the session<br>to be held as soon as possible and no later than three weeks<br>from the date of receiving the referral. ## Permanency Planning

CONCURRENT PLANNING

Legal Basis

H612 Section 1.13(b) (effective 10/1/2025) amends N.C.G.S. § 7B-906.2(b) states that at any permanency planning hearing, the court shall adopt one or more of the following permanent plans the court finds is in the juvenile's best interest: Reunification, Adoption, Guardianship, Custody, APPLA, or Reinstatement of Parental Rights. Concurrent planning shall continue until (i) a permanent plan is or has been achieved or (ii) reunification is not identified as a permanent plan (primary or secondary).

At any permanency planning hearing where the court is ordering reunification as a permanent plan, the court shall adopt concurrent permanent plans and shall identify the primary plan and secondary plan. Reunification shall be a primary or secondary plan unless the court relieved the department of making reunification efforts at initial disposition under G.S. 7B-901(c), previously made written findings under made findings under G.S. 7B- 906.1(d)(3), the permanent plan is or has been achieved, or the court makes written findings that reunification efforts clearly would be unsuccessful or would be inconsistent with the juvenile's health or safety. The finding that reunification efforts clearly would be unsuccessful or inconsistent with the juvenile's health or safety may be made at any permanency planning hearing, and if made, shall eliminate reunification as a plan. When reunification has been eliminated as a permanent plan (primary or secondary), concurrent planning is not required. Unless permanence has been achieved, the court shall order the county department of social services to make efforts toward finalizing the primary and secondary permanent plans and may specify efforts that are reasonable to timely achieve permanence for the juvenile.

When a juvenile is not being reunified with a parent, guardian, or custodian, prior to any change in placement for the juvenile, the department shall file a motion before the court and request that a hearing be held within 30 days when all of the following criteria exist:

(1) The juvenile is in the custody of a county department of social services.

(2) The juvenile has resided with the caretaker for the preceding 12 consecutive months, and the caretaker objects to the removal.

(3) The current caretaker is one of the following individuals:

a. A relative caretaker.

b. A nonrelative caretaker, and there are no relatives who are willing and able to provide proper care and supervision of the juvenile

in a safe home.

(4) The court-ordered primary or secondary permanent plan is adoption.

(5) The current caretaker objects to the removal and has notified the department of their desire to adopt the juvenile.

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

<!-- page: 151 -->
## Permanency Planning

The clerk shall give notice of the hearing to the parties, the parties' attorneys, and the current caretaker. The department of social services shall either:

• Provide to the clerk the name and address of the juvenile's current caretaker for notice under this subsection or

• File written documentation with the clerk that the juvenile's current caretaker was sent notice of hearing.

The court shall provide the current caretaker the opportunity to address the court, present evidence, cross-examine witnesses, and be represented by an attorney at the caretaker's own expense. Nothing in this subsection shall be construed to make the current caretaker a party to the proceeding. The court may consider any evidence, including hearsay evidence as defined in G.S. 8C-1, Rule 801, or testimony or evidence from any person that is not a party, that the court finds to be relevant, reliable, and necessary to determine the needs of the juvenile. At the hearing to review the change of placement, the court shall determine whether it is in the best interests of the juvenile to be removed. This subsection shall not apply to cases when there are allegations of abuse or neglect of the juvenile while under the care and supervision of the current caretaker.

Definition:

CONCURRENT PLANNING Concurrent permanency planning is the process of working towards a primary permanent plan of for a child, such as reunification, while developing at least one alternative, or secondary, permanent plan at the same time. Concurrent planning is used to keep the focus on the child's urgent needs for safety and permanence and to reduce the length of time a child spends in county child welfare services agency custody. When reunification has been eliminated as a permanent plan (primary or secondary), concurrent planning is not required (H612 Section 1.13(b) amending N.C.G.S. § 7B-906.2(b)).

Concurrent planning ensures a secondary plan is developed if efforts to achieve the primary plan are unsuccessful. A secondary permanency plan is developed, and efforts to achieve the primary and secondary plan are made concurrently. If the primary plan is unsuccessful, the secondary plan has been developed and can be fully implemented. It is not inconsistent to work toward reunification while building a case which will support concurrent planning and alternative resolutions.

When a child enters county child welfare services agency custody, the primary plan is usually reunification with the parents or caretakers from whom the child was removed. In concurrent planning, the county child welfare worker is developing at least one secondary permanent plan jointly with the family.

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Permanency Planning

### PERMANENCY PLANNING HEARING

Legal Basis

N.C.G.S 7B-906.1 (a) states when custody has been removed from a parent, guardian, or custodian, the hearing shall be designated as a permanency planning hearing. The court shall conduct a permanency planning hearing within 90 days from the date of the initial dispositional hearing. Permanency planning hearings shall be at least every six months thereafter to review the progress made in finalizing the permanent plan for the juvenile, or if necessary, to make a new permanent plan for the juvenile.

N.C.G.S § 7B-906.1 (b) states that the director of social services shall make a timely request to the clerk to calendar each hearing at a session of court scheduled for the hearing of juvenile matters. The clerk shall give 15 days' notice of the hearing and its purpose to (i) the parents, (ii) the juvenile if 12 years of age or more, (iii) the guardian, (iv) the person providing care for the juvenile, (v) the custodian or agency with custody, (vi) the guardian ad litem, and (vii) any other person or agency the court may specify.

According to P.L. 113-183, with each permanency hearing held with respect to the child, the agency shall document the intensive, ongoing and, as of the date of the hearing, unsuccessful efforts made by the agency to return the child or secure a placement for the child with a fit and willing relative (including adult siblings), a legal guardian, or an adoptive parent, including thorough efforts that utilize search technology (including social media) to find biological family members for children.

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

## Child and Family Team / Permanency Planning Review Meetings

Legal Basis

Sec. 475. [42 U.S.C. §675] of the Social Security Act states that the status of each child is reviewed periodically by a panel of appropriate persons, at least one of whom is not responsible for the case management of, or the delivery of services to, either the child or the parents who are the subject of the review. The purpose of the review is to determine the safety of the child, the continuing necessity for and appropriateness of the placement, the extent of compliance with the case plan, and the extent of progress which has been made toward alleviating or mitigating the causes necessitating placement in foster care, and to project a likely date by which the child may be returned to and safely maintained in the home or placed for adoption or legal guardianship and, for a child for whom another planned permanent living arrangement has been determined as the permanency plan, the steps the county child welfare services agency is taking to ensure the child's foster family home or child care institution is following the Reasonable and Prudent Parent Standard and to ascertain whether the child has regular, ongoing opportunities to engage in age- or developmentally-appropriate activities (including by consulting with the child in an age-appropriate manner about the opportunities of the child to participate in the activities).

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|PERMANENCY PLANNING REVIEW TEAM MEETINGS<br>For children who are in the legal custody or placement responsibility<br>of a county child welfare services agency, a PPR must be held at the<br>following required intervals:<br>•<br>Within 60 days of the child coming into agency custody or<br>placement responsibility; and<br>•<br>Every 90 days thereafter throughout the life of the case; and<br>•<br>When there is a recommended change in the permanent<br>plan outside of the regular review schedule.<br>PPRs are open, non-adversarial forums for focusing on casework<br>practice and planning. The PPR process allows each party involved to<br>have input into service needs of the child and family; to document<br>progress of the parents in improving the conditions that led to<br>county child welfare services agency custody; to develop the most<br>appropriate permanent plan; and to ensure permanency is achieved<br>for every child.|PPRs should be action-oriented. These reviews are valuable in achieving a<br>safe, permanent home for every child in the legal custody or placement<br>responsibility of a county child welfare services agency within one year. While<br>these reviews are needed to discuss the child, family, and agency efforts, they<br>also ensure that every county child welfare services agency custody case<br>moves quickly toward a permanent resolution.<br>There are many variations of when a permanency planning review will need to<br>be held outside of the regular review schedule. Additional reviews should be<br>held based on the needs of the family and any significant change in<br>circumstances. |help the family achieve the goals identified in the<br>Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement; and/or<br>•<br>The extent of compliance with the Permanency Planning<br>Family Services Agreement.<br>PARTICIPANTS IN THE PPR<br>Participants who must be invited to the PPR include (but are not<br>limited to) the following:<br>•<br>The child's parent(s), unless parental rights have been<br>terminated;<br>•<br>The child, if appropriate;<br>•<br>The child's placement provider;<br>•<br>Natural supports identified by the family;<br>•<br>Community resource persons, at least one of whom is not<br>responsible for the case management or delivery of services<br>to the child or parents; and<br>•<br>The GAL<br>If reunification is no longer the primary plan, any identified<br>permanent placement resources (custodian/guardian/adoptive<br>parent) must be invited to participate in the PPR.<br>Parent Participation:<br>Parents have the right to participate in every PPR of their child's<br>case. Parents must be provided timely written notice of team<br>meetings and every reasonable effort must be made to meet at a<br>time and location that enables the parents' attendance.|Children/youth should always be consulted as to whom they would like to<br>have on their team; this is especially important if the birth parents are no<br>longer attending the meetings. The child should have a voice at the meeting<br>and should be encouraged to share their wishes for their future. Decisions<br>made at PPRs should be made "through the eyes of the child." The more<br>agencies can empower children by including them in the decision-making<br>process, the better those agencies serve them. One of the individuals selected<br>by the child may be designated to be the child's advisor and, as necessary,<br>advocate, with respect to the application of the Reasonable and Prudent<br>Parent Standard to the child.<br>It is considered appropriate for the child to participate in a PPR meeting if the<br>child is of sufficient age and maturity, and it is developmentally-appropriate<br>for the child to be present.<br>Foster parents and other placement providers have the most current and<br>complete knowledge of the child's adjustment in foster care. They play a vital<br>role in the planning and decision-making regarding the child's future. They<br>should always be strongly encouraged to attend and participate fully in the<br>Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement planning and review<br>meetings.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Child and Family Team / Permanency Planning Review Meetings

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|The agency must notify the parent(s) of:<br>•<br>Their right to attend and present information from their<br>perspective;<br>•<br>Issues that will be discussed;<br>•<br>The date and location of the meeting; and<br>•<br>The right to have an attorney present.<br>NOTE: If the parent(s)/caretaker(s) are not willing or able to<br>participate in a PPR within the required timeframes, the Permanency<br>Planning Family Services Agreement must still be updated.<br>Child/Youth Participation:<br>The child must be consulted in an age-appropriate manner about<br>any permanency plans for the child. If a youth has obtained the age<br>of 14 years or older, by federal law the child must be consulted and<br>given the opportunity to select up to two members as part of the<br>team who are not a foster parent of, or CW caseworker for, the<br>child.<br>Signatures of all persons attending the PPR meeting must be<br>documented.|Parents should be encouraged to bring relatives, kin, or any other support<br>person they would like to have present at the meeting. A broad definition of<br>family should be used when considering who should be a part of the PPR.<br>By providing services to children and/or their families, community resource<br>providers may have information essential to planning and decision-making. It<br>is crucial to involve them in the planning and review process. The child's<br>teachers and/or guidance counselors should be included in this process.<br>In addition, having at least one resource person who has no direct service or<br>case management responsibilities to the case strengthens case decision-<br>making. Not only does this provide for additional input into the child's case,<br>but an individual with no direct case responsibility is better able to view the<br>"big picture" objectively and make recommendations from the broader<br>community perspective.<br>Community resource persons with no direct case management responsibility<br>can include, but are not limited to the following:<br>•<br>Mental health representative;<br>•<br>School representative;<br>•<br>Healthcare provider/representative;<br>•<br>Fatherhood initiative representative; or<br>•<br>Social services representative such as a Work First or economic<br>services worker, if the representative has no knowledge or association<br>with the case.<br>The child's GAL can bring a different perspective to the case review.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Child and Family Team / Permanency Planning Review Meetings

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|CHILD AND FAMILY TEAM MEETINGS (CFTS)<br>For children who are in the legal custody of a county child welfare<br>services agency, a CFT meeting must be held when there is a:<br>•<br>Change in placement (or potential change in placement) of<br>the child;<br>•<br>Change in family circumstance and it is necessary to<br>reconvene the team to discuss the case, this includes when<br>there is a change in the juvenile's school;<br>•<br>Change in the permanent plan; or<br>•<br>Any time the family or child request the team be convened.<br>H612 Section 1.13(b) (effective 10/1/2025)amending N.C.G.S. § 7B-<br>906.2(b): When a juvenile is not being reunified with a parent,<br>guardian, or custodian**, prior to any change in placement for the**<br>**juvenile**, the department shall file a motion before the court and<br>request that a hearing be held within 30 days when all of the<br>following criteria exist:<br>(1) The juvenile is in the custody of a county department<br>of social services.<br>(2) The juvenile has resided with the caretaker for the<br>preceding 12 consecutive months, and the caretaker objects<br>to the removal.<br>(3) The current caretaker is one of the following individuals:<br>a. A relative caretaker.<br>b. A nonrelative caretaker, and there are no relatives<br>who are willing and able to provide proper care and supervision of<br>thejuvenile in a safe home.|A Child and Family Team meeting must be scheduled when there is a change<br>in placement or potential change in placement. This meeting should occur**as**<br>**soon as possible**and address the following:<br>•<br>Is the juvenile able to remain safely in their current placement?<br>•<br>What factors have been identified contributing towards placement<br>disruption?<br>•<br>What resources and supports may help sustain the placement?<br>•<br>If a placement change is inevitable, what information regarding the<br>juvenile can be shared to assist the new placement provider?<br>•<br>Has the agency received input from the juvenile's biological family<br>regarding the child's needs?<br>•<br>Does the child's mental health provider have recommendations<br>regarding placement?<br>•<br>If there will be a change in placement**, **a Best Interest Determination<br>meeting (BID) can occur during the CFT. An educational professional<br>must be present if the BID meeting occurs during the CFT. Depending<br>on family circumstances and desires the BID may need to be<br>scheduled separately from the CFT meeting.<br>When there has been a change in the permanent plan, there must be a CFT.<br>The county child welfare agency should:<br>•<br>Clearly communicate the new primary plan and concurrent plan (if<br>required) to all those impacted by the plan change.<br>•<br>Discuss the next action steps towards achieving permanency.<br>•<br>The Permanency Planning Family Service Agreement must be updated<br>within 30 days of the court's decision to change the child's permanent<br>plan. The county child welfare agency should provide an opportunity<br>for the Permanency Planning Family Services Agreement to be<br>developed or updated during a CFT meeting, or individually at a later<br>time if family preferred.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|

|**Termination of Services**<br>PREPARING THE CHILD<br>County child welfare agencies must help prepare the<br>child for an exit from county child welfare services<br>agency custody, no matter the permanent plan being<br>achieved.<br>For youth exiting foster care at age 18 the agency must:<br>•<br>Provide important documents to the youth prior<br>to the exit;<br>•<br>Complete the appropriate Transitional Living Plan<br>(TLP); and<br>•<br>Discuss the option of participating in Foster Care<br>18 to 21with the youth.|<br>Whether a child has been in county child welfare services agency custody for a short or a<br>long period of time, the move out of care is equally as significant as the move into care.<br>The child may have conflicting feelings about the change in living arrangements. It is the<br>county child welfare worker's responsibility to help him/her express and understand these<br>conflicting feelings and to move gradually toward making the change. The county child<br>welfare worker should plan with the child, age appropriately about the kinds of<br>responsibilities the child can take in getting ready for the move.<br>Whether a child is being discharged from family foster care, relative placement, or from<br>institutional care, the caretaker should plan with the county child welfare worker for the<br>move and participate in preparing the child for the changes. Changes in living<br>arrangements usually mean changes in relationships. If it is appropriate, the child may<br>need to visit their former placement after discharge.<br>Services may be extended by the agency to youth ages 13 to 21 who leave foster care and<br>meet the eligibility requirements for LINKSservices, regardless of their living arrangement<br>and whether the agency retains custody or placement responsibility of the youth during<br>this time. This extension of services will allow the county child welfare worker to provide<br>needed support to the youth after discharge from foster care placement. The services<br>should be based upon an assessment of the youth's needs, to assist him/her in making a<br>successful transition to living independently.<br>The child's life book should be given to the child and/or the parent(s)/guardian/custodian/<br>adoptive family.|

<!-- page: 163 -->
# Termination of Services

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|PREPARING THE FAMILY FOR REUNIFICATION<br>The agency must request that visitation between the<br>child and parent(s) increase, including unsupervised<br>visitation and a trial home visit.<br>The agency must also comply with the requirements of<br>Rylan's Law/CPS Observationprior to recommending<br>reunification occur.<br>The county child welfare services agency must provide<br>the family with any important documents and other<br>items pertaining to the child including, but not limited<br>to:<br>•<br>Medical records;<br>•<br>Medications; and<br>•<br>School records.|The child and family have changed during the time of placement. Even over a matter of<br>months, the child will have achieved developmental milestones, will have formed new<br>relationships with foster parents, and may have new interests. Families will have adjusted<br>their daily routines around the absence of the child. Parents may have learned new<br>parenting skills that impact familiar family practices.<br>During the planning process, the county child welfare worker should keep the child and<br>family abreast of the changes that are occurring. When placement providers are<br>encouraged to work with the birth families, both the child and the family can benefit from<br>a significant increase in the amount of information shared.<br>As the family moves toward reunification, the county child welfare worker should be very<br>sensitive to the fears of the family. They may be afraid they are not ready for the child's<br>return and could lose their child again. The county child welfare worker should work with the<br>family to assure needed supports are in place. Family Preservation Services may be included<br>during the trial home visit or as part of the aftercare plan to further stabilize the family.<br>County child welfare agencies should aid with transitioning Medicaid and other services the<br>child is receiving, when appropriate.|
|PREPARING THE FOSTER FAMILY<br>The foster family must participate in planning for the<br>child's exit from county child welfare services agency<br>custody.<br>The foster family must assist in transitioning the child to<br>their permanent living arrangement.|The foster family needs the county child welfare worker's full support and recognition of<br>the contributions they have made in the child's life. The foster family should be informed<br>of why the county has reached a decision to move a child to a permanent placement. Such<br>information and preparation will help the foster family come to an acceptance and<br>understanding of these events, so they can help a child adjust to the move. If it is in the<br>best interest of the child, a contact between the child and the foster family should be<br>arranged by the agency after a child has moved to a more permanent placement.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

# Termination of Services

| Protocol - What you must do | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|
|PREPARING THE ADOPTIVE FAMILY OR OTHER PERMANENT CAREGIVER<br>If the adoptive family or other permanent caregiver has not lived with the child, the agency must<br>arrange for a transitional period of visitation to help the child and family learn about each other.<br>The adoptive family or other permanent caregiver must be provided with all information that is<br>relevant to the child's history, relationships, behaviors, health, interests, and educational needs.<br>Non-identifying information about the child's birth family must be provided to the adoptive family<br>so the child will be able to know the reason for their adoption.<br>The agency must make post-adoption services available to every adoptive family. These services<br>must be provided to facilitate the integration of the child and family and to resolve problems they<br>may encounter. The agency must provide regular and ongoing support, monitoring, and/or<br>counseling of the family as appropriate. A referral to Family Preservation Services may be<br>appropriate forpost-adoption services.|PREPARING THE ADOPTIVE FAMILY OR OTHER PERMANENT CAREGIVER<br>If the adoptive family or other permanent caregiver has not lived with the child, the agency must<br>arrange for a transitional period of visitation to help the child and family learn about each other.<br>The adoptive family or other permanent caregiver must be provided with all information that is<br>relevant to the child's history, relationships, behaviors, health, interests, and educational needs.<br>Non-identifying information about the child's birth family must be provided to the adoptive family<br>so the child will be able to know the reason for their adoption.<br>The agency must make post-adoption services available to every adoptive family. These services<br>must be provided to facilitate the integration of the child and family and to resolve problems they<br>may encounter. The agency must provide regular and ongoing support, monitoring, and/or<br>counseling of the family as appropriate. A referral to Family Preservation Services may be<br>appropriate forpost-adoption services.||
|AFTERCARE SERVICES<br>The agency must inform families and children that they can request services from the agency and<br>obtain these services on a voluntary basis, as available.<br>Families must be informed of services that may be available to them including, but not limited to:<br>•<br>Adoption assistance;<br>•<br>Post-adoption support;<br>•<br>Guardianship assistance;<br>•<br>LINKS services and funding; and<br>•<br>Foster Care 18 to 21 services.|AFTERCARE SERVICES<br>The agency must inform families and children that they can request services from the agency and<br>obtain these services on a voluntary basis, as available.<br>Families must be informed of services that may be available to them including, but not limited to:<br>•<br>Adoption assistance;<br>•<br>Post-adoption support;<br>•<br>Guardianship assistance;<br>•<br>LINKS services and funding; and<br>•<br>Foster Care 18 to 21 services.|The agency should establish a minimum period<br>for providing supportive aftercare services and<br>supervision to the child and their permanent<br>family. In most cases, agency supervision can be<br>terminated after six months unless the court<br>orders otherwise and/or the final Risk<br>Reassessment indicates additional service needs.<br>Guardians and legal custodians should also be<br>made aware of available services, so they can<br>select services they need. If they indicate no need<br>for services, they should be informed that<br>services are available to them, should they need<br>them later.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

### Assessing a Minor for Transition to Adult Guardianship Services

| Legal Basis:<br>• [Chapter 35A - NC Statutes]: Establishes formal procedures for incompetency hearings.<br>• Federal law: Fostering Connections Act (2008) and Title IV-E funding support extended care for young people with disabilities.<br>• [G.S. 108A-48(c)]: Permits extended foster care to age 21 under qualifying conditions, including incapacity.<br>• [G.S. 131D-10.2B]: Allows children in foster care who have reached the age of 18 to continue to receive foster care services until they reach the age<br>of 21.<br>Adult Guardianship Services are provided to individuals who are alleged to be incompetent or adjudicated incompetent by the court. For adults who have<br>been adjudicated incompetent by the court, directors or assistant directors of local Department of Social Services often serve as guardians. Initiating the<br>proceeding before a person's eighteenth birthday enables a guardian to be appointed and assume responsibility when the person becomes eighteen; this<br>prevents a gap in decision-making authority for the person which may otherwise occur if the proceeding is not initiated until the person's eighteenth<br>birthday.<br>Guardianship Services Include:<br>• Assessing an individual's need for guardianship<br>• Petitioning or assisting the individual's family for the adjudication of incompetence and the appointment of a guardian<br>• Ongoing casework when the agency director or assistant director has been appointed as guardian<br>Responsibilities of the Guardian Include:<br>• Making decisions about where the individual will live<br>• Authorizing medical treatment, managing the individual's finances<br>• Filing status reports and accountings with the court | |
|---|---|
|Protocol - Whatyou must do|Guidance - Howyou should do it|
|N.C. Gen. Stat. § 35A-1201 (2023) Guardian and Ward<br>This statute outlines the purpose and guiding principles of North Carolina's<br>adult guardianship system under Chapter 35A. It emphasizes protecting the<br>rights, dignity, and well-being of individuals who are adjudicated<br>incompetent and require assistance managing personal or financial affairs.<br>The statute promotes the least restrictive alternative, meaning that any<br>guardianship arrangement should interfere as little as possible with the<br>person's autonomy while still providing for the person's safety and well-<br>being.<br>The statute expressly acknowledges that county Departments of Social<br>Services (DSS) may serve as public guardians of last resort when no one else<br>is willing or able to serve; it recognizes their authority within the larger|Before initiating a petition for adjudication of incompetence for a person<br>who meets criteria in the applicable statutory definition of incompetency,<br>careful consideration should be given as to whether the person's need for<br>assistance can be met through other means. Proceedings for adjudication of<br>incompetence and appointment of a guardian should be initiatedonly when<br>other less restrictive forms of intervention for an incompetent person are<br>determined to be inappropriate or inadequate.The appropriate use of<br>alternatives to guardianship depends on the nature of the incompetent<br>person's abilities and limitations, income and resources. Some alternatives to<br>guardianship and examples of circumstances in which each may be<br>appropriate include:<br>(1) Appointment of a payee to handle financial benefits coming from a<br>government agency.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL
