|guardianship system and situates DSS's role within the broader goals of<br>protecting incapacitated individuals and ensuring their care and rights are<br>maintained.<br>Guardianship Petition Filing and Court Process<br>A local county DSS may file a written request (a petition) with the clerk of<br>superior court alleging that the client/respondent should be declared<br>incompetent (§ 35A-1210. Application before clerk). Every clerk's office has<br>forms that may be completed and filed for the petition. The petition must<br>include a sworn statement that the information in the petition is true.<br>When a petition is filed, the clerk of court sets a date and time for the<br>guardianship hearing. The sheriff serves copies of the petition and notice of<br>the hearing on the client/respondent and on his or her attorney or other<br>representative. Before the hearing, the clerk may order medical,<br>psychological, social work and other evaluations of the client/respondent. At<br>the hearing, the clerk serves as judge. The legal determination that the<br>client/respondent is incompetent means that he or she is considered<br>incapable of making important decisions concerning his or her personal<br>welfare and/or financial resources.<br>Once the client/respondent has been determined to be incompetent and a<br>guardian(s) is appointed, the guardian will receive a written order of<br>appointment from the clerk. This order explains the guardian's powers and<br>duties.<br>For more information on this process, visit the NCDHHS Guardianship<br>webpage or refer to the NC DSS Guardianship Manual.|(2) The Adult Protective Services law (G.S. 108A, Article 6) to authorize<br>provision of needed essential services on a short-term basis.<br>For more information on these alternatives to adult guardianship, refer to<br>the NC DSS Guardianship Manual.<br>Timeline for Assessing a Minor for Transition to Adult Guardianship<br>Services<br>No later than 12 months before the young person in foster care turns 18:<br>• The Child Welfare (CW) caseworker and supervisor identify the<br>young person as a potential candidate for adult guardianship, based<br>on a review of their mental and physical capacity to manage<br>personal welfare and financial affairs.<br>• CW initiates contact with adult services (AS), including Foster Care<br>18-21 caseworker.<br>• AS caseworker is assigned.<br>No later than 9 months before the young person turns 18:<br>• An internal case staffing meeting occurs during which staff:<br>o Assess completed evaluations and determine any that are<br>still required.<br>• Assess the supportive services that are needed and those that are<br>already in place.<br>• Case staffing should include:<br>o CW caseworker and supervisor<br>o AS caseworker and supervisor<br>o Foster care 18-21 caseworker<br>o County DSS attorney<br>No later than 6 months before turning 18:<br>• CW and AS caseworkers jointly initiate/schedule Transition Team<br>meeting (in person or virtual) to discuss guardianship. At this<br>meeting, the CW and AS caseworkers should co-lead a discussion<br>about guardianship to gather input and reach a consensus regarding<br>whether to pursue guardianship.<br>• The Transition Team meeting should include:<br>o CW caseworker<br>o AS caseworker|

| | o Foster care 18-21 caseworker<br>o GAL volunteer (for the juvenile case)<br>o The impacted young person (whenever possible)<br>o Members of the young person's familial or community<br>support network<br>o Placement provider<br>o Members of the young person's physical and behavioral<br>health support networks (as applicable)<br>• The transition team should develop a written guardianship plan<br>which describes the young person's mental and physical condition,<br>recommended type of guardianship, scope of the guardian's<br>authority to best meet the person's needs without assuming any<br>more control than necessary, and the most appropriate party to<br>serve as guardian (see NC DSS Guardianship Manual for more detail).<br>• If the Transition Team meeting concludes that guardianship is the<br>most appropriate supportive option for the young person, the AS<br>caseworker is responsible for initiating the guardianship process<br>through the clerk of court and updating the Transition Team of their<br>progress.<br>Simultaneously, the CW caseworker is responsible for:<br>• Ensuring the young person is signed up for relevant supportive<br>services and benefits.<br>• Supporting the maintenance of the young person's support network.<br>After 18<br>• Following the guardianship process, the AS caseworker is responsible<br>for post-18 adult case management.<br>• When the agency director has been appointed guardian,<br>guardianship services involve the provision of services to the ward,<br>ward's family, and others involved with the ward's care, comfort,<br>maintenance and over-all well-being. On-going case management<br>involves goal setting, planning services/treatment, monitoring and<br>reassessing the service/treatment plans, and termination of the<br>guardianship, where appropriate. |Potential Conflicts of Interest in Foster Care 18-21 and Adult Guardianship<br>Cases<br>In situations where a young adult in the Foster Care 18-21 program also<br>requires adult guardianship, county DSS agencies must navigate the overlap<br>of continuing support services and legal authority with care. While<br>participation in both systems is permitted, certain actions may present a<br>conflict of interest and should be handled with procedural safeguards. For<br>more information, please refer to this August 2024 DCDL or the DSS<br>Guardianship Manual.<br>1. Participation in the Foster Care 18-21 Program<br>a. Young adults in foster care at age 18 may continue receiving<br>benefits and services until age 21, regardless of medical<br>condition, disability, or ability to meet education or<br>employment requirements. For more information, see the<br>Foster Care 18 to 21 section.<br>b. It is not a conflict of interest for a young adult to participate|Key Considerations (for caseworkers)<br>• Early Identification & Engagement<br>o What are the methods/strategies used to identify young<br>people who may benefit from adult guardianship support?<br>o Have you discussed adult guardianship options with the<br>young person and their supports?<br>• Are there people who may be willing to assume the role of<br>guardianship when the young person becomes an adult?<br>• Warm Hand-Off + Inter-Agency Role Clarity<br>o What opportunities are there to connect the young person<br>with an adult services caseworker?<br>o What are the best strategies for ensuring a warm handoff<br>when transitioning a young adult who needs adult<br>guardianship from their child welfare caseworker to an adult<br>services caseworker?<br>• Least Restrictive Alternatives & Placement Stability<br>o Is it possible to keep a young person in their current<br>placement beyond the age of 18?<br>o What community or day programs exist?<br>• Benefit Coordination<br>o Have you explored available federal, state, and community<br>benefits and resources?<br>Recommendations for County Best Practice<br>• Establish internal review protocols for any case where DSS both<br>holds guardianship and provides 18-21 services.<br>• Promote use of limited guardianship in cases where the young adult<br>can meaningfully participate in legal agreements. A limited<br>guardianship should be recommended when the adult has the<br>capacity to make and communicate decisions in certain areas and<br>not in other areas. Refer to the Adult Services Guardianship Manual<br>for more information on limited guardianship.<br>• Avoid situations where the same DSS representative evaluates a<br>young person's eligibility, petitions for guardianship, and signs legal<br>agreements on the young person's behalf.<br>Consider collaboration with neighboring counties or regional legal support to<br>provide independent guardianship representation where needed.|

| in a county's Foster Care 18-21 program when that same<br>county DSS holds guardianship if the young adult has limited<br>guardianship and signed their own Voluntary Placement<br>Agreement.<br>c. These services are often best delivered by staff who are<br>familiar with the young adult's history and transition needs.<br>2. Voluntary Placement Agreement (VPA) and Guardianship<br>a. A conflict of interest arises when the DSS Director (or their<br>designee) acts in dual roles—both as the guardian and the<br>signatory on the VPA.<br>b. In accordance with best practices, a DSS Director (or their<br>designee) acting as guardian should not sign the VPA on<br>behalf of the young adult. At this point, the case should be<br>treated as a conflict case and transferred to a partnering<br>county to provide either FC 18-21 services or Guardianship<br>should the young adult benefit from participating in both FC<br>18-21 VPA and Guardianship. A decision should be made<br>based on the client's best interest.<br>3. Promoting Autonomy through Limited Guardianship<br>a. If the young adult has capacity to understand and consent to<br>legal agreements, efforts should be made to pursue limited<br>guardianship, preserving the young adult's choice to sign the<br>VPA independently. This ensures the young adult's voice<br>remains central in decisions that affect their future and<br>avoids unnecessary conflicts in representation.<br>4. Statutory Clarification<br>a. Under North Carolina General Statute Chapter 7B, the term<br>"director" includes designated representatives of the DSS<br>agency. Therefore, any individual acting on behalf of the<br>director is subject to the same conflict-of-interest<br>considerations. | |
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

### Documentation and Record Keeping

|Protocol - What you must do|Guidance - How you should do it|
|---|---|
|Documentation and record keeping are critical in managing a permanency planning case.<br>Documentation and record keeping must be maintained to meet federal, state, and local<br>mandates.<br>**MAINTENANCE OF THE PERMANENCY PLANNING RECORD**<br>The electronic NC FAST record for each child in the custody of a county child welfare services<br>agency must be maintained throughout the life of the case.|Documentation and record keeping should be<br>viewed as a valuable tool for the county child<br>welfare worker and child welfare supervisor, as<br>well as for the court.<br>The NC FAST electronic record should provide and<br>maintain a history of the child and family's<br>involvement.<br>Electronic records in NC FAST should include<br>documents that constitute legal evidence. A<br>comprehensive and up-to-date record will alert the<br>county child welfare worker and the child welfare<br>supervisor when required action is needed in the<br>case, such as agency case reviews, court reviews,<br>and contacts with the child, family, and others.|
|**CONTENTS OF THE PERMANENCY PLANNING RECORD**<br>Case documentation must be current within 7 working days.<br>Documentation in the electronic record must include:<br>•<br>A description of the actions taken;<br>•<br>Current progress toward the goal and objectives stated on the Permanency Planning<br>Family Services Agreement(DSS-5240);<br>•<br>A current copy of the Child Placement and Payment Report(DSS-5094); and<br>•<br>The rationale for agency involvement and services delivery on an ongoing basis.<br>The agency must maintain an individual electronic record for each child or sibling group in NC<br>FAST. Each electronic record must contain:<br>•<br>Demographic information, which must include:<br>`o` The name, address, sex, race, ethnicity, Social Security Number, date of birth, and||

# Youth And Young Adult Services: NC LINKS

| Legal Basis | |
|---|---|
|In December of 1999, the U.S. Congress enacted the John Chafee Foster Care Independence Act(42 USC § 675) and established the John H. Chafee Foster Care<br>Independence Program. This Act made substantial changes in the federal efforts that target current and former youth in the foster care system.<br>The law was amended in 2002 to include Education and Training Vouchers for youth aging out of foster care and youth who are adopted or exit to guardianship<br>after their sixteenth birthday. This program is described later in this manual.<br>In 2018, the Family First Prevention Services Act renamed the program to be the Chafee Foster Care Program for Successful Transition to Adulthood (the Chafee<br>program). The State of North Carolina refers to the Chafee Program as NC LINKS.|In December of 1999, the U.S. Congress enacted the John Chafee Foster Care Independence Act(42 USC § 675) and established the John H. Chafee Foster Care<br>Independence Program. This Act made substantial changes in the federal efforts that target current and former youth in the foster care system.<br>The law was amended in 2002 to include Education and Training Vouchers for youth aging out of foster care and youth who are adopted or exit to guardianship<br>after their sixteenth birthday. This program is described later in this manual.<br>In 2018, the Family First Prevention Services Act renamed the program to be the Chafee Foster Care Program for Successful Transition to Adulthood (the Chafee<br>program). The State of North Carolina refers to the Chafee Program as NC LINKS.|
|Policy|Policy|
|The NC Foster Care Independence Program (NC LINKS) is a state-funded initiative that supports youth ages 14* to 21—currently or formerly in foster care—as<br>they transition to adulthood.<br>**NC LINKS**is not an acronym— it is a word that captures the purposes of the Chafee Act and the intent of NC: to build a network of relevant services with youth<br>so that they will have ongoing connections with family, friends, mentors, the community, employers, education, financial assistance, skills training, and other<br>resources to facilitate their transition to adulthood.<br>*Youth aged 13 years old may be eligible for LINKS services. See _Youth, Age 13 _section for more information_._|The NC Foster Care Independence Program (NC LINKS) is a state-funded initiative that supports youth ages 14* to 21—currently or formerly in foster care—as<br>they transition to adulthood.<br>**NC LINKS**is not an acronym— it is a word that captures the purposes of the Chafee Act and the intent of NC: to build a network of relevant services with youth<br>so that they will have ongoing connections with family, friends, mentors, the community, employers, education, financial assistance, skills training, and other<br>resources to facilitate their transition to adulthood.<br>*Youth aged 13 years old may be eligible for LINKS services. See _Youth, Age 13 _section for more information_._|
|Protocol- Whatyou must do|Guidance|
|**Links Eligibility**<br>1. Youth and Young Adults eligible for LINKS funding:<br>2. Youth in foster care, ages 14 to 21<br>3. Young adults currently or formerly in foster care, at ages 18-21<br>4. Youth who left foster care through adoption or guardianship (including<br>KinGAP) at age 16 or older<br>5. Youth "likely to age out of foster care" (see Youth, Age 13)|**Links Eligibility**<br>Eligibility for LINKS services is intentionally broad, allowing agencies to<br>support youth and young adults who need help and are willing to take<br>an active role in addressing their challenges. Caseworkers should work<br>closely with each youth, their supporters, and service providers to<br>identify personalized goals and steps toward self-sufficiency. Programs<br>should remain flexible, as long as they align with the core goals of<br>LINKS.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|6. Youth who exit foster care after the age of 14 through reunification of a<br>parent or primary caretaker; custody with non-removal parent or relative; or<br>custody with other court approved caretaker remain eligible for LINKS<br>services and funding.<br>Definition of Foster Care and Eligibility for LINKS<br>For LINKS policy purposes, a youth is considered to be in foster care if they have been<br>removed from their home and placed in 24-hour substitute care under the custody<br>and placement responsibility of a county child welfare agency (CWA). Youth placed<br>voluntarily in DSS custody by a parent without agency involvement may also be<br>considered in foster care. Youth who exited foster care as teens—whether through<br>reunification, adoption, placement with relatives, marriage, or emancipation—<br>remain eligible for LINKS services until age 21.<br>Note: Facilities primarily used for juvenile detention (e.g., detention centers, training<br>schools, forestry camps) are not considered foster care placements. Youth who have<br>only been in such facilities and never in foster care are not eligible for LINKS services<br>or funding.<br>Participation Requirement<br>To receive LINKS services or funding, youth must actively participate in their<br>transitional planning. This includes helping design and carry out their case plan.<br>Services cannot be provided to youth who refuse to engage in planning or take<br>responsibility for their goals.<br>Youth/Young Adults Ineligible for LINKS Funding:<br>The primary focus of the LINKS program is to serve youth and young adults who are<br>eligible to receive these funds. Youth and young adults are ineligible to receive LINKS<br>funds under the following circumstances:<br>• Youth who are not yet 14 years of age (Some exceptions may apply. See<br>Youth, Age 13)<br>• Youth receiving CPS Investigations, Family Assessments, or CPS In-Home<br>services, unless they were previously in foster care after the age of 14.|Youth/Young Adults Ineligible for LINKS Funding:<br>There is a distinction between LINKS funding and LINKS services. While<br>a youth/young adult may be ineligible to receive LINKS funding, there<br>may be circumstances which allow them to still receive LINKS services.|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

| • Undocumented, non-U.S. citizen youth (NOTE: If legal residency is<br>established, the youth would become eligible for LINKS funds.)<br>• Ineligible siblings of LINKS participants<br>• Youth with personal reserves of $10,000 or more<br>However, county CW agencies may provide services to a youth/young adult ineligible<br>for LINKS funding if:<br>• The LINKS Coordinator deems the services necessary and appropriate; and<br>• The provision of services involves no additional cost to the agency, or, if the<br>services to individual does involve additional cost, those costs will be paid<br>through other sources (family, private donations, county funds, etc.); and<br>• The provision of services does not displace an eligible youth/young adult.<br>If providing services to a youth/young adult that is ineligible for LINKS funding:<br>• For a youth/young adult in agency custody:<br>o Document the requested service in the Permanency Planning<br>Family Case Plan (PPFCP) and clearly indicate how the cost of the<br>service will be covered, since LINKS funds cannot be used.<br>• For a youth/young adult not in agency custody:<br>o Document the requested service and the justification for providing<br>it in the youth/young adult's casefile; specify how the cost will be<br>managed within the LINKS program or through other available<br>resources. | |

|**Links Services**<br>LINKS services should support participation in activities that are age and<br>developmentally appropriate.<br>LINKS Services for Youth Age 13:|**Links Services**<br>To best serve all eligible youth, county CW agencies should consider<br>utilizing the following strategies:|

|Youth who are 13 years old and in foster care may be eligible for LINKS funding or<br>services to support their engagement in age-appropriate or developmentally<br>appropriate activities.<br>For these youth, county CW agencies must:<br>• Staff the youth's case with LINKS services and funds for approval by the State<br>LINKS Coordinator;<br>• Evaluate the youth's need for assistance and access to participate in<br>appropriate activities;<br>• Assess and document the likelihood that the youth will remain in foster care<br>through age 18; and<br>• Confirm the youth's need for LINKS services and funding, and submit a<br>request for county reimbursement of Special LINKS funds from the State, as<br>needed.<br>LINKS Services for Youth Ages 14-17:<br>For youth in foster care between the ages of 14 and 17, county CW agencies must:<br>• Assess life skills;<br>• Develop a Transitional Living Plan (TLP);<br>• Determine appropriate life skills training and skill development activities;<br>• Identify services related to educational and/or vocational needs;<br>• Locate resources to support employment;<br>• Determine the need for formal counseling and informal personal support;<br>and<br>• Develop and nurture personal support networks.<br>LINKS Services for Young Adults Ages 18-21:<br>Young adults in foster care under a Voluntary Placement Agreement (VPA) must be<br>offered life skills training, counseling, education, and other services to support their<br>transition to self-sufficiency. Agencies are responsible for teaching essential skills and<br>providing opportunities to practice them in a supportive environment.<br>Due diligence is required of county CW agencies to identify, locate, and offer services<br>to young adults who:<br>• Are participating in the Foster Care 18 to 21 Program;|• Engage the broader community in providing supportive learning and<br>living environments for youth and young adults who have<br>experienced foster care.<br>• Co-sponsor a group with another agency that serves teenagers.<br>• Co-sponsor a group, event, or activity with another county LINKS<br>program to maximize resources.<br>• Utilize online resources that allow youth, on their own time, to<br>explore their interests, develop skills, and plan for their post-<br>secondary or vocational education and career.<br>• Encourage youth to participate in skills-based courses through<br>schools, agricultural extension programs, recreation programs,<br>community resource centers, and other resources.<br>For more information on age and developmentally appropriate activities<br>review the Reasonable and Prudent Parent Activities Guide.<br>Young Adults ages 18-21:<br>Outreach services should include:<br>• Diligent, persistent, and ongoing efforts to locate and contact<br>young adults who age out of foster care at least ONCE a year;<br>• Once located, an assessment of the young adult's current<br>situation, barriers that they are experiencing, efforts they have|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|• Were discharged from agency custody at age 18;<br>• Are not enrolled in the Foster Care 18 to 21 Program;<br>• Are under the age of 21; and<br>• May need referrals or financial assistance for services and resources.<br>For outreach purposes, due diligence refers to reasonable efforts to identify, locate,<br>assess the needs of, and inform young adults of services for which they may be<br>eligible.<br>LINKS Services for Young Adults Who Aged Out of Care:<br>Young adults who aged out of foster care (i.e., were in care on their 18th birthday)<br>must be offered any eligible services they may need. Counties may be reimbursed<br>through:<br>• LINKS Housing Funds: For rent, security deposits, or room and board<br>arrangements.<br>• LINKS Transitional Funds: For non-housing expenses such as utilities,<br>furniture, and other essentials.<br>Priorities for Service Delivery<br>Under 42 USC § 477 of the Social Security Act, the State is mandated to ensure that<br>Native American/Indigenous children have equal access to LINKS benefits and<br>services. NC interprets this obligation to include all Native American children,<br>regardless of their federal recognition status.<br>N.C.G.S. § 143B-139.5A. formalized collaboration between local CW agencies and<br>Indian tribes on child welfare matters, including the provision of LINKS services to<br>Native youth in foster care. Special attention must be given to identifying American<br>Indian youth who qualify for LINKS and to removing any barriers that may hinder<br>their participation. A key focus of LINKS services for Native youth should be to<br>support and preserve their cultural connections to their tribes.|made to overcome those barriers, and plans or strategies for<br>agency assistance, if requested;<br>• Engagement of young adults in planning, determination of what<br>responsibility the young adults will handle, and choice of<br>services that will supplement their own efforts.|

|Involvement in Service Provision<br>Caretakers:<br>Caretakers and providers must support youth in making developmentally appropriate<br>decisions regarding participation in various activities. These may include, but are not<br>limited to, extracurricular enrichment and social opportunities such as outdoor<br>recreation, socializing with peers, dating, prom-related events, sports, clubs,<br>vocational programs, religious or family gatherings, field trips, driver's education,<br>and other age-appropriate experiences. For more information, visit the Reasonable<br>and Prudent Parent Activities Guide.<br>Youth/Young Adults:<br>Youth/young adult involvement in planning individualized services is required.<br>Youth/young adults must be afforded every opportunity to actively participate in<br>identifying their needs and associated services, and in the development of their<br>Transitional Living Plan (TLP).<br>The recommendations made by the youth/young adults must be incorporated to the<br>extent that the Child and Family Team (CFT) or Permanency Planning Review (PPR)<br>agrees that such recommendations are in the youth's best interest, and that these<br>comply with applicable policies and statutes, and do not adversely affect the safety<br>or well-being of the youth or others. It is appropriate for the CFT and PPR members<br>to discuss the possible consequences of not accepting recommended action steps<br>with youth/young adults.<br>Note: Youth 13 years and older have the legal right to be involved in the<br>development of the service plans if they are cognitively and emotionally able to<br>participate. Agencies are encouraged to have young adults participate and have an<br>active role in planning meetings.|Involvement in Service Provision<br>Youth/Young Adults:<br>An effective way to demonstrate openness to youth/young adults in the<br>planning process is to encourage them invite family, mentors, friends, or<br>other supporters to their service plan reviews. This arrangement<br>accomplishes several goals, including:<br>• Giving youth/young adults ownership in the planning process<br>• Offering significant people in the youth/young adult's life an<br>opportunity to engage in the planning process<br>• Helping the agency identify support people that may have been<br>previously unknown to them<br>Confidentiality concerns should be addressed by having participants sign<br>a confidentiality form that the participants are aware that information<br>shared during the meeting is confidential, and that they agree not to<br>divulge any information shared without the expressed permission of the<br>youth/young adult.|

<!-- page: 181 -->
|Links as an Outcome-Based Service<br>LINKS uses assessment tools to help youth identify their life skills, goals, and<br>motivation. With support from a LINKS Coordinator, each youth creates a<br>personalized Transitional Living Plan (TLP) that builds on strengths and addresses<br>needs in areas like education, employment, and housing. Guided by the goals of the<br>Federal Chafee Program, NC LINKS aims for all youth who experienced foster care as<br>teens to achieve key outcomes by age 21. All services must be tailored to support<br>progress toward one or more of these outcomes. Also see LINKS as an Outcome-<br>Based Service Resource Guide.<br>Outcome #1: Young adults have Sufficient Economic Resources to Meet Daily<br>Living Needs<br>• If a young person is a dependent child, the family provides sufficient<br>resources for basic daily living needs.<br>• Job stability for youth or young adults, as indicated by at least six months<br>in the same job during the previous year.<br>• The youth or young adult pays own portion of daily living expenses or<br>contributes to an agreed-upon amount.<br>• If unable to pay own portion of expenses, the young person has stable<br>and consistent income/resources, such as SSI, or stable financial support,<br>that is sufficient to pay his bills.|Links as an Outcome-Based Service<br>Outcome #1 Examples:<br>• Job readiness skills;<br>• Conflict resolution skills;<br>• Vocational interest testing;<br>• Volunteer work for younger and older youth in their fields of<br>interest;<br>• Competency-based training for the work environment, money and<br>time management, credit management, conflict management,<br>personal conduct in a work environment, tax preparation, etc.;<br>• Experiences preparing for employment while in custody, such as<br>part-time employment, participation in school-to-work programs,<br>job sharing or apprenticeships, and job coaching;<br>• Part-time or full-time employment for youth 16 and older;<br>• Young adult's responsibility for saving own money and paying part<br>of their personal expenses;<br>• Driver's education and driver's license or identification card issued<br>by the state in accordance with the requirements of section 202 of<br>the REAL ID Act of 2005 before discharge;<br>• Vocational Rehabilitation testing and training, when appropriate;<br>and<br>• Transitional education and training resources such as Job Corps,<br>AmeriCorps, college or vocational training, military options,<br>WIA/JobLinks, etc.|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 174 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 182 -->
|Outcome #2: Young adults have a Safe and Stable Place to Live<br>• The young adults are living in a stable housing situation and can afford the<br>cost;<br>• The housing situation is not hazardous and is not in high-crime area; and<br>• The young person has not been victimized since discharge.|Additional or alternative program elements for young adults with<br>unrealized potential to support themselves completely (those continuing<br>their education/training, currently unemployed, or temporarily disabled)<br>and who need temporary financial support:<br>• Family/extended family/community support;<br>• Public assistance, e.g. TANF, Food Stamps, Work First, Section 8, or<br>other public housing, etc.;<br>• Vocational Rehabilitation;<br>• Educational/Vocational scholarships and grants as well as other<br>postsecondary school assistance; and<br>• Foster Care 18 to 21/Voluntary Placement Agreement with the<br>local CWA while attending school full time.<br>Program elements for young adults with disabilities or developmental<br>delays may include:<br>• Life skills training to help young adults develop greater<br>independence;<br>• Eligibility determination for SSI and Medicaid;<br>• Extended family/community support;<br>• Public assistance;<br>• Sheltered workshops/supported employment programs;<br>• Supported housing programs;<br>• CAP-MR/DD adult programs; and/or<br>• Assisted living programs for adults.<br>Outcome #2 Examples:<br>• Learning about housing/utility costs for various types of housing<br>in the intended home community;<br>• Learning to evaluate safety; using police and crime statistics,<br>news reports, etc., as resources to learn about the location of<br>safe neighborhoods;|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|Outcome #3: Young adults are Attaining or have Attained Academic or Vocational<br>Educational Goals in Keeping with their Interests or Abilities<br>• The young person is on or above grade level or, if not, is receiving assistance<br>to attain grade level.<br>• The young person's stated educational and vocational goals are openly<br>explored.<br>• Education/vocational training is reasonable given level of<br>academic/vocational ability and interests.<br>Outcome #4: Young adults have Connections to a Positive Personal Support System<br>• The young person has a broad-based personal support system of at least 5<br>adult supporters who are not related to the agency and who are personally<br>interested and invested in the youth's future.|• Learning to enhance personal safety in the home including basic<br>knowledge about home repair and avoiding common hazards;<br>• Learning to avoid danger and to defuse volatile interpersonal<br>situations;<br>• Learning basic non-lethal self-protection techniques;<br>• Having a stable place to live upon discharge, with a primary and<br>backup discharge plan to minimize the likelihood of<br>homelessness resulting from a disrupted plan;<br>• Having trusted friends who can offer temporary sanctuary, if<br>needed; and<br>• Making concerted efforts toward permanence for every youth<br>prior to discharge, including learning about adult adoption<br>procedures.<br>Outcome #3 Examples:<br>• Proactive remedial academic assistance for youth who are not<br>achieving grade level: educational testing, tutoring, computer-<br>based learning, vocational interest/ability testing, tutoring based<br>on academic deficits, etc.;<br>• Early exposure to a variety of academic/vocational schools and<br>possible means to attend those schools;<br>• Positive reinforcement for achievement of steps toward<br>personal academic and/or vocational goals- recognition,<br>rewards, privileges, etc.; and<br>• Developing strong working relationships between the local<br>CWA and the public school to establish in-school mentors and<br>advocates for participating youth.<br>Outcome #4 Examples:<br>• Helping all young adults to seek out lifetime connections and<br>permanence while in care and beyond; providing information on<br>adult adoptions to youth and adults that are in family-like<br>relationships;|

|Authentic Youth Engagement and Positive Development<br>Authentic youth engagement builds individual leadership skills, strengthens<br>meaningful relationships, and leads effective solutions. Authentic youth engagement<br>happens when young adults are actively and authentically involved, motivated, and<br>excited about an issue, process, event, or program. The role of the LINKS Coordinator<br>is grounded in these concepts.<br>Positive Youth Development (PYD) is a comprehensive framework that outlines the<br>support that all young adults need to be successful. This approach engages youth<br>within their environment in a manner that is productive and constructive; PYD<br>recognizes, utilizes, and enhances young adult's strengths and promotes positive<br>outcomes for young adults. For more information, visit the Positive Youth<br>Development Resource Guide. https://policies.ncdhhs.gov/divisional-n-z/social-<br>services/child-welfare-services/cws-policies-manuals/<br>The Positive Youth Development approach to LINKS services works with youth to<br>improve their assets, agency, enabling environment, and contribution. A key<br>component of this approach is ensuring normalcy—providing youth in foster care<br>with developmentally appropriate opportunities and experiences that are typical for<br>their age and stage, similar to those of their peers not in foster care.<br>County CW agencies must design and deliver LINKS programming and services that:<br>o Assess youth to determine their strengths or assets;<br>o Support youth in exercising independence- employing their assets and<br>critical thinking skills to make decisions about their lives and set their own<br>goals;<br>o Create or allow opportunities for youth to experience growth-enhancing<br>interactions within their environment;<br>o Promote normalcy by supporting participation in extracurricular activities,<br>social events, and everyday life experiences that foster a sense of belonging<br>and autonomy;|Authentic Youth Engagement and Positive Development<br>To authentically engage youth, the LINKS Coordinator should embody the<br>roles of a teacher, coach, and advocate.<br>• Teacher: As a teacher, the LINKS Coordinator works with youth to<br>determine what he or she needs to know to proceed toward self-<br>sufficiency. As a teacher, the LINKS Coordinator is alert to planned<br>and spontaneous opportunities that will encourage positive youth<br>development.<br>• Coach: Coaching includes on-the-spot teaching as well as allowing<br>the experiences of the youth to do the teaching and encouraging<br>youth to figure out their own solutions.<br>• Advocate: As an advocate, the LINKS Coordinator not only speaks on<br>behalf of youth but also seeks opportunities for youth to advocate<br>for themselves. As an advocate, the LINKS Coordinator may work<br>diligently to keep agency staff aware about the program, and help<br>the agency understand youth strengths and abilities, rather than<br>making decisions solely focused on problems.<br>NOTE: Normalcy is the goal of allowing children to participate in typical<br>childhood activities, while the reasonable and prudent parent standard is<br>the decision-making framework used by caretakers (especially in foster<br>care) to permit these activities. Normalcy includes, but not limited to,<br>extracurricular, enrichment, and social activities, and may include<br>overnight activities outside of the direct supervision of the caregiver over<br>a period of 24 and up to 72 hours. See the Reasonable and Prudent<br>Parent Activities Guide for more information.<br>For more information, see Authentic Youth Engagement Resource Guide.|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|**Responsibilities of the Links Coordinator**<br>While the structure of a LINKS program may vary by county, all NC counties are<br>mandated to offer LINKS services and assign a LINKS Coordinator to deliver these<br>required services to eligible youth and young adults.<br>Key responsibilities of the county LINKS Coordinator:<br>•<br>Relationship Building:Develop a good working relationship with eligible<br>youth and young adults, their caregivers, supporters, and caseworkers, using<br>a positive youth development approach;<br>•<br>Assessment of Life Skills: Required for all participants to determine the youth<br>or young adult's strengths and skills as well as the need for additional<br>training or experiences. For more information, see the ASSESSMENT OF LIFE<br>SKILLSsection of this manual.<br>•<br>Transitional Life Plan Development:Collaborate with youth and required<br>participants to develop a Transitional Living Plan (TLP)that target key areas<br>to be addressed before the youth becomes an adult.<br>•<br>Program Management:Work cooperatively with eligible county youth and<br>young adults to develop and conduct a relevant and effective county LINKS<br>program;<br>•<br>Budget and Funding Management:<br>`o` Develop budget and annual plan for the operation of the county<br>LINKS program;<br>`o` Verify eligibility for LINKS and LINKS Special Funds, ensure that<br>ineligible youth and young adults are not served using additional<br>federal IV-E funds, and ensure that LINKS funds are not used to cover<br>costs that other state or federal funding sources are earmarked to<br>cover;|**Responsibilities of the Links Coordinator**<br>To support the professional development of the LINKS Coordinator and<br>participate in sustaining county LINKS programs, LINKS Coordinators<br>should:<br>•<br>Participate in regional training and meetings with state LINKS<br>Coordinators and other counties as schedule allows;<br>•<br>Participate in monthly conference calls with county coordinators,<br>state coordinator and partners, as schedule allows;<br>•<br>Ensure the transportation for county youth to attend regional<br>events and participate actively with attending youth and other<br>adults,<br>•<br>Meet with coordinators from other counties to consolidate or<br>coordinate services as appropriate.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

| o Submit requests for reimbursement of Special Funds to the state<br>LINKS Coordinator on behalf of county. Ensure that expenditure of<br>the LINKS funds is allowed;<br>o For more information, see the FUNDING section of this manual.<br>• Outreach and Resource Referrals: Conduct diligent outreach efforts to all<br>young adults, ages 18-21 who aged out of foster care in the county and<br>provide appropriate services to these young adults; Refer eligible young<br>adults to the Education and Training Voucher Program and the NC Reach Program;<br>• Advocacy and Support: Advocate creatively for youth and young adults and<br>encourage youth to be self-advocates and leaders;<br>• Data Management: Respond to requests for data from the State Coordinator;<br>• Professional Development: Participate in LINKS training offered through<br>NCDHHS DSS Staff Development (LINKS 101, and other courses as<br>appropriate);<br>• Consult with the State Coordinator, as needed. | |

|**Assessment of Life Skills**<br>Assessing the youth's life skills is critical to determining the youth or young adult's<br>strengths and skills, as well as the areas for additional training or experiences. Life<br>Skills Assessments must be completed with all youth and young adults, ages 14 or<br>older, who are in foster care or foster care, 18 to 21 years old.<br>Assessment of Life Skills by Age Group<br>**Assessment of Youth Ages 14-17:**Life Skills Assessments for youth ages 14 to 17<br>must involve both the youth, CW worker, and a person who knows the youth's skills<br>and abilities first-hand, such as a family member or caregiver. For youth ages 14-17,<br>the assessment must be completed:<br>•<br>Within 30 days of entering foster care, if over 14 years old;<br>•<br>Within 30 days of 14th birthday;|**Assessment of Life Skills**<br>Assessing the life skills of youth/young adults enables the LINKS<br>Coordinator and involved CW caseworkers to engage the youth/young<br>adult in learning opportunities that may include teaching and leadership<br>programs. Proper use of life skills assessment tools provides a roadmap<br>for identifying skills and resources that the youth/young adult may need<br>to achieve their goals.<br>Assessment of Life Skills by Age Group<br>**Assessment of Youth, Ages 14-17**should be completed using a formal<br>assessment, as selected by the agency.|

|• Every 12 months thereafter; and<br>• As needed, based on Transitional Living Plan goal achievement, observed<br>progress in life skill development, or when there is a significant change in<br>circumstances or desired goals of the young person.<br>Assessment of Young Adults Ages 18-21: Life Skills Assessments for young adults<br>ages 18 to 21 must involve the young adult, CW worker, and, as requested by the<br>young adult, a support person the young adult trusts with first-hand knowledge of<br>their skills and abilities. For young adults ages 18-21, the assessment must be<br>completed:<br>• Within 30 days of entering foster care, 18 to 21 years old;<br>• Every 12 months thereafter; and<br>• As needed, based on Transitional Living Plan goal achievement, observed<br>progress in life skill development, or when there is a significant change in<br>circumstances or desired goals of the young adult.<br>For more information on available Life Skills Assessments see the LINKS Resource<br>Guide for Life Skills Assessments https://policies.ncdhhs.gov/divisional-n-z/social-<br>services/child-welfare-services/cws-policies-manuals/.|Assessment of Young Adults, Ages 18-21 may be completed using a<br>formal assessment tool, as selected by the agency. However, the focus<br>for young adults who seek LINKS services is typically about one or more<br>life crises that they are struggling to handle. In these situations, informal<br>assessments conducted during an interview or discussion are often<br>needed. Such discussion should be documented per their case record<br>and inform the young adult's Transitional Living Plan.|

|**Transitional Living Plans (TLP)**<br>Purpose and Overview<br>The Social Security Act (42 U.S.C. § 675) requires states to have a plan for foster care,<br>which must include a Transitional Living Plan (TLP) to prepare youth for independent<br>living. Furthermore, the Fostering Connections to Success and Increasing Adoptions<br>Act of 2008 (P.L. 110351) expanded foster care eligibility to age 21 in participating<br>states and required that no more than 90 days before a youth's exit from the foster<br>care system, a TLP must be developed with the youth a specific plan for his or her<br>transition to independent living.|**Transitional Living Plans (TLP)**<br>Purpose and Overview<br>Transitional Living Plans (TLPs) are structured, youth-centered planning<br>tools designed to prepare young adults in foster care for a successful<br>transition to adulthood and independence. The planning process should<br>be youth-driven, reflective of familial values, and inclusive of supportive<br>adults and professionals.For more information regarding TLPs, see the<br>LINKS Transitional Living Plan Resource Guide.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 184 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 192 -->
|Transitional Living Plan (TLP) Requirements<br>TLPs are required for youth in foster care beginning at age 14 and are updated<br>regularly to reflect the youth's evolving goals, needs, and circumstances. TLPs are<br>developed collaboratively with the youth and their support network and are<br>grounded in the results of a Life Skills Assessment. TLP must be developed, reviewed,<br>and updated according to specific timelines.<br>Transitional Living Plan for 14-17 Year Olds<br>For youth ages 14-17 in foster care, a TLP (DSS-5096a) must be developed within 30<br>days (and reviewed within 60 days) following the youth's 14th birthday, or when the<br>youth enters foster care, if age 14 or older; and updated every 90 days thereafter.<br>See Instructions Help Sheet (DSS-5096ains) for more information.<br>Emancipation Plan for 17-21 Year Olds<br>The Emancipation Plan for 17-21 year olds (DSS-5096b) must be completed during<br>different timeframes depending on the age of the young person (Young adults aged<br>17 or Young adults aged 18-21 with a planned exit). The Emancipation Plan is<br>considered an extension of the TLP, as it provides details and resources for the youth<br>after he or she exits care.<br>This document is required for all young adults in foster care within 90 days prior to their<br>18th birthday. The young adult must be informed of his/her option to continue in the<br>Foster Care 18 to 21 Program at the time the 90 Day Transition Plan is completed. See<br>Instructions Help Sheet (DSS-5096bins) for more information.<br>Transitional Living Plan for Youth/Young Adults Who Have Left Foster Care<br>The Transitional Living Plan - Part C - SHORTENED (DSS-5096c) is optional and should<br>only be used only for LINKS eligible youth who are not currently in either foster care<br>or Foster Care 18-21.|Transitional Living Plan (TLP) Requirements<br>These timelines ensure that youth receive timely and ongoing support in<br>planning for their future.<br>Emancipation Plan for 17-21 Year Olds<br>The Emancipation Plan captures details about the young adult's<br>resources - where they plan to live after exiting from foster care, their<br>level of educational attainment, their educational or vocational plans<br>and goals, transportation, health insurance - and provides additional<br>resources for the young adult should they need additional supports<br>upon exiting care. The 90 Day Transition Plan document also verifies<br>the young adult has received the required documents prior to exiting<br>care.<br>The Emancipation Plan meeting for young adults approaching their 18th<br>birthday should be structured as a Child and Family Team (CFT) Meeting<br>(see Cross Functions Manual for more info on CFTs). Youth may invite<br>their family and other support persons to help finalize this plan.<br>Although the Emancipation Plan must be completed 90 days prior to a<br>youth's exit from foster care, counties are encouraged to begin<br>preparation and engage the youth in the planning process well in<br>advance of this deadline. Caseworkers should meet with the youth early<br>on to explain the purpose of the plan, outline the youth's role in its<br>development, and identify individuals the youth may wish to invite to|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 185 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 193 -->
|Transitional Living Plan for Young Adults in Foster Care 18-21<br>For young adults participating in the Extended Foster Care 18 to 21 Program, a<br>Transitional Living Plan (TLP) (DSS-5096d) must be developed within 30 days following<br>the young adult's 18 birthday or within 30-days of their entry into the program. This<br>plan must be reviewed and updated every 90 days. If the young adult resides out of<br>state, the form may be sent to the young adult via email for signature or the young<br>adult may provide a verbal acknowledgement, which must be documented by the<br>case worker.<br>Advance Health Care Directive<br>Under the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act (P.L. 111-148), Transition Living<br>Programs (TLPs) are required to include information in their transition planning for<br>youth aging out of foster care or independent living programs about the importance<br>of establishing a health care power of attorney. This planning must emphasize the<br>value of designating a trusted individual to make medical decisions on behalf of the<br>youth in the event they are unable to do so themselves, particularly if they do not<br>have or do not wish to appoint a relative, as would otherwise be designated under<br>NC law.<br>In NC, individuals may file such documents with the Advance Health Care Directive<br>Registry, maintained by the Secretary of State. The registry provides access to<br>standardized forms that comply with state law and allow individuals to formally<br>appoint a health care agent to act on their behalf in medical matters.<br>Required Documentation<br>The 2018 Families First Prevention Services Act (Public Law 115-123) Section<br>475(5)(I)) requires county CW agencies to provide official documentation to prove|planning sessions to help represent their needs. These discussions<br>should also clarify how the Emancipation Plan aligns with and differs<br>from the Transitional Living Plan (TLP), and what key topics will be<br>addressed—such as housing, health insurance, transportation,<br>education, and employment.<br>Developing a comprehensive 90-Day Transition Plan may require multiple<br>planning sessions due to the range of topics involved. Counties may find<br>it beneficial to leverage LINKS support groups and/or SaySo to gather<br>youth-led input on what information and preparation are most helpful<br>prior to formal transition planning. These activities and<br>recommendations can empower youth to take an active role in shaping<br>their own emancipation plans. Additionally, caseworkers should prepare<br>referral information—including contact details—for agencies and<br>services based on where the young adult plans to live after discharge.<br>Advance Health Care Directive<br>Counties must ensure that information is provided that assists the youth<br>in clearly understanding that the individual designated as their health<br>care agent will have broad powers to make health care decisions for the<br>youth when the youth is unable to make those decisions or cannot<br>communicate their decision to other people. It is important that the<br>youth and the person they designate as their health care agent discuss<br>the youth's wishes concerning life-prolonging measures, mental health<br>treatment, and other health care decisions. Except for any specific<br>limitations or restrictions expressed, the youth's health care agent may<br>make any health care decision the youth could make for themselves.|
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

| the young adult was in foster care before they age out of care. Per Public Law 113-<br>183 and Public Law 115-123, the agency must make arrangements to have the<br>following documents available to give to the young adult at the time of the<br>meeting:<br>• Verification Letter - Proof of Child's Placement in CW<br>• Original or certified copy of the youth's birth certificate<br>• A Social Security card issued by the Commissioner of Social Security<br>• All copies of all required Health Forms<br>• Initial Provider Assessment (DSS-5203)<br>• Comprehensive Provider Assessment (DSS-5204)<br>• Health Summary Form (DSS-5206)<br>• Child Health (DSS-5207)<br>• All medical records<br>• Health insurance information<br>• Education Status forms (DSS-5245)<br>• Driver's license or identification card issued by the State in accordance with<br>the requirements of section 202 of the REAL ID Act of 2005<br>• Copies of any credit reports and documentation related to issues resolved<br>on the credit report<br>• The original signed copy of the TLP and 90 Day Transition Plan<br>• Transition Checklist<br>The entire 90 Day Transition Plan should be copied for agency files. The original<br>plan as well as original or certified documents should be provided to the youth in a<br>permanent binder to take from the meeting. The young adult must sign the<br>Transition Checklist to validate receipt of the items identified above.<br>Copies of the discharge documents shall be retained in the youth's record.<br>Reimbursement from the county LINKS allocation or LINKS Special funds may be<br>used to pay for the required documents, costs of copying, and a protective portable<br>file in which these documents may be stored. | |

|**Permanency for Adolescents**<br>Development of Permanent Placement Resources<br>Successful adoption recruitment strategies for teens are very different from<br>those directed at locating families foryounger children. Traditional adoption||

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 196 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 204 -->
| recruitment efforts tend to attract families seeking to adopt infants and<br>young children. If those same strategies are used to attempt to attract families<br>interested in adopting teens, the rate of success is minimal. There are logical<br>reasons for this:<br>• Teens in foster care are, first and foremost, teenagers. Average teens from<br>average families are at a developmental stage that is typified by pulling away<br>from family, not by joining into a family.<br>• Teens in foster care are unfairly prejudged as being emotionally disturbed,<br>delinquent, violent, and/or generally incapable of being part of a family.<br>• Many adoption recruitment workers would rather put their energies into<br>getting more younger children adopted, rather than focusing on the few<br>teens who need adoptive placement.<br>• Most families would not consider adopting a teen unless they knew him or her<br>personally.<br>• Most teens would not consider being adopted, unless they knew the potential<br>adoptive parent personally.<br>• Many teens resist the concept of adoption for a variety of reasons: they may<br>not want to change their name (although they do not have to do so), or they<br>may feel that accepting adoption means that they would not see their<br>relatives again, (although this may be worked out). They may feel that they<br>do not deserve a permanent home. They may be concerned that if they<br>agree to adoption, no family would step forward to provide a permanent<br>home for them. They do not want to be rejected again.<br>• Many agencies do not make a concentrated effort to help teens to create<br>new connections with caring adults, nor do they ask the youth to help them<br>identify adults that they already know and trust. | |
|---|---|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

| General Recruitment for Permanent Homes<br>As with all recruitment efforts, outreach should be directed toward communities<br>whose backgrounds, experiences, or characteristics align with those of the youth we<br>are seeking to support. Educational presentations should be held to advertise the<br>need to find permanent homes for teens and preteens. The specified purpose of<br>these events is to encourage the community to help to prevent homelessness of<br>teens exiting the system. We know that youth leaving the foster care system as<br>adults without strong personal support systems are much more likely to face<br>homelessness than those who do have these relationships. During these<br>presentations prospective adoptive parents should be given an opportunity to hear<br>directly from adolescents in foster care and to learn about the type of "parenting"<br>that is needed by these teens.<br>Advertising for these events are distributed where the people are likely to see<br>them: supermarket bulletin boards, newspapers and media, leaflets in<br>restaurants, announcements in churches, mosques, and synagogues, within tribal<br>organizations, etc.<br>Youth-Specific Recruitment<br>All teens in foster care have some emotional attachments to others in order to have<br>survived. They have created their own "families." These "families" may consist of<br>friends, parents of friends, current and/or former foster parents, teachers, coaches,<br>cottage parents, maintenance staff, relatives, older siblings or friends who are now<br>adults, neighbors, church members, Guardians ad Litem, caseworkers, employers,<br>counselors, etc. We need to ask these youth about these connections and to help<br>them strengthen these relationships. There are often more than a dozen people<br>currently in each youth's life that could be approached about offering a permanent<br>home to the youth. Asking youth to invite persons of their own choosing to<br>participate in their planning reviews helps us to know who some of these people<br>are. | |
|---|---|

## Foster Care 18 to 21

Legal Basis In 2008, the Fostering Connections to Success and Increasing Adoptions Act (H.R. 6893/P.L. 110-351) allowed states to receive federal Title IV-E reimbursement for costs associated with supports for young adults to remain in foster care up to age 21. In 2015, the North Carolina General Assembly revised N.C.G.S. §108A- 48 to extend the provision of benefits under this statute to young adults between 18 and 21 years of age effective January 1, 2017.

In addition, N.C.G.S. § 131D-10.2B provides authority under which young adults who turn 18 in foster care can continue to receive foster care services until the age of 21 through the Foster Care 18 to 21 program. Policy Foster Care 18 to 21 is a program that offers young adults the opportunity to continue foster care placement and transition to independence with county child welfare agency (CWA) supervision. There are some young adults who may not be ready to exit foster care upon turning 18 years of age and may choose to stay in Foster Care 18 to 21 as long as certain requirements are met. Young adults who exit foster care at age 18 are also able to return to the Foster Care 18 to 21 program at a later date as long as they have not reached their 21st birthday.

County CW agencies are responsible for providing eligible young adults who enter into an agreement for Foster Care 18 to 21 services with ongoing efforts to help prepare and ensure the young adult's successful transition into adulthood. This policy is the basis for providing foster care benefits and services to young adults, as well as eligibility requirements, development of the Voluntary Placement Agreement (VPA), county oversight, required court hearings, and placement options for young adults receiving services.

NOTE: Young adults who choose to participate in Foster Care 18 to 21 are eligible to receive services and funding from NC's LINKS program. A young adult who is otherwise eligible for Foster Care 18 to 21 cannot be required to participate in Foster Care 18 to 21 to gain access to LINKS services and funding.

Legal Responsibility for Placement Young adults receiving Foster Care 18 to 21 services are adults for all purposes, except the continued provision of foster care services. These young adults need the same degree of independence and decision making that other young adults over age 18 are granted. Unless a court has appointed a guardian, participants are their own guardians. A young adult who is at least 18 years of age but less than 21 years of age and was in the custody of a county CWA upon reaching his or her 18th birthday, must enter into a Voluntary Placement Agreement with the county CWA in order to receive Foster Care 18 to 21 services. The court will hold an initial review hearing and may periodically review placement and services for young adults receiving services.

|**Foster Care 18 to 21 Eligibility**<br>Program Criteria:<br>A young adult who has reached 18 years of age but is not yet 21 years of age, and was in<br>foster care upon their 18th birthday, may continue to receive foster care benefits and<br>services up to 21 years of age as long as**one of the following criteria**is met on an ongoing<br>basis:<br>•<br>Enrolled and regularly attending secondary education (high school) or a<br>program leading to an equivalent credential; or<br>•<br>Enrolled and regularly attending an institution that provides postsecondary or<br>vocational education; or<br>•<br>Regularly participating in a program or activity designed to promote or<br>remove barriers to employment; or<br>•<br>Regularly employed for at least 80 hours per month; or<br>•<br>Incapable of completing the educational or employment requirements due to a<br>medical condition or a disability.<br>NOTE: Young adults who initially choose to opt out of foster care upon attaining 18 years of<br>age may choose to receive Foster Care 18 to 21 services at a later date as long as they have<br>not reached their 21st birthday, and they meet at least one of the program eligibility<br>requirements listed above.|**Foster Care 18 to 21 Eligibility**<br>High School or Equivalent / Post-Secondary or Vocational<br>Education_: _"Regularly attending" identifies a young person<br>complying with the attendance policies of their education<br>program. This sets the expectation that while unexpected<br>barriers occur, forward progress towards self-sufficiency is<br>being made, no matter how slow.<br>Programs/Activities Designed to Promote or Remove Barriers<br>to Employment_: _"Regularly participating" identifies a young<br>person complying with the expectations of their program.<br>This sets the expectation that while unexpected barriers<br>occur, forward progress towards self-sufficiency is being<br>made, no matter how slow.<br>Programs and activities that fall into this category include, but<br>are not limited to:<br>•<br>Vocational Rehabilitation or Workforce Innovation and<br>Opportunity Act (WIOA) programs and services<br>•<br>Unemployment Benefits due to job loss<br>•<br>Online career exploration or other programming to build<br>skills and/or search for employment<br>•<br>Volunteer work<br>•<br>Internships or apprenticeships<br>•<br>Job Corps: Any combination of working, volunteering, and<br>participating in programming to remove barriers to<br>employment and build skills to make up the difference in<br>hours if the young adult is unable to meet the 80- hour a<br>month employment criterion for eligibility.<br>•<br>If determined necessary by a provider, intensive mental<br>health services such as an Assertive Community Team<br>(ACT).|

To establish need, the young adult must meet the definition<br>of "needy" based on his or her income and resources. As long as the young adult's<br>countable income does not exceed 100% of the need standard and meets the<br>$10,000 resource limit, the young adult is considered to meet the financial need.<br>• Age: The young adult must be age 18, 19, or 20 and meeting one of the eligibility<br>criteria for Foster Care 18 to 21.<br>• Placement and Other Requirements:<br>o The young adult must be the placement responsibility of a county<br>CWA and must be residing in an eligible setting.<br>o The young adult must not be residing in a locked detention facility.<br>o The young adult must have an individualized Transitional Living Plan which<br>includes goals and activities that the young adult is working towards<br>o The young adult must be a U.S. citizen or a "qualified alien."<br>Since there are no ongoing funding eligibility requirements, other than meeting one of the<br>eligibility criteria, verification of continued participation in the program should be<br>documented in the record and communicated with the Medicaid department and any<br>other individuals involved with the young adult any time there is a change in eligibility and<br>at least every 12 months. | |

|**Healthcare Eligibility**<br>The Affordable Care Act (ACA) extends Medicaid coverage to youth aging out of foster care<br>on or after their 18th birthday, to age 26. Individuals who turn age 18 while in foster care<br>continue to be eligible for Medicaid. Consult Medicaid for additional rulings. To qualify,<br>young adults must meet all of the following criteria:<br>•<br>Age 18 to 26<br>•<br>In foster care at age 18 and enrolled in NC Medicaid<br>•<br>A resident of NC<br>•<br>Not receiving Medicaid from any other state<br>•<br>Not an inmate of a public institution<br>All young adults aging out of foster care must be referred to their local Medicaid office to<br>apply for continuing healthcare coverage.|**Healthcare Eligibility**<br>Due to The Substance Use-Disorder Prevention that Promotes<br>Opioid Recovery and Treatment for Patients and Communities<br>Act, enacted in 2018, effective January 1, 2023, youth formerly<br>in foster care will be eligible for Medicaid until age 26 regardless<br>of which state they currently reside. |**Credit Checks**<br>County CW agencies are responsible for assisting young adults receiving Foster Care 18 to<br>21 services with obtaining his or her credit report once every year until reaching 21 years<br>of age. Young adults<br>also have the right to refuse this service. |**Transitional Living Plan (TLP)**<br>Purpose: The Transitional Living Plan (TLP) is a tool to develop individualized goals that will<br>assist the young adult in transitioning into self- sufficiency. It also includes specific<br>information the young adult will need in preparation of exiting Foster Care 18 to 21. The<br>TLP must be developed in partnership with the young adult and individuals identified as his<br>or her supports.<br>The goals and anticipated outcomes on the TLP will ensure the young adult's active and<br>meaningful participation in one or more of the eligibility requirements for Foster Care 18<br>to 21. It will also outline independent living services, document the young adult's<br>approved placement setting, include a plan for transitioning into adulthood and living<br>independently, and provide steps the young adult, caseworker, and other support team<br>members are taking to ensure the young adult achieves independent living, including but<br>not limited to:<br>housing, education and/or employment, money management, and maintaining permanent<br>connections to caring and committed adults. The TLP will also include a fully developed<br>backup plan in the event of an unplanned break in participation, as well as a transition plan<br>for young adults approaching their 21st birthday, or a planned exit from Foster Care 18 to<br>21.<br>For information on the development and maintenance of Transitional Living Plans, see<br>Required Services for Children.<br>90 DAY TRANSITION PLAN<br>The Fostering Connection to Success and Increasing Adoptions Act of 2008 (Sec. 202 of<br>the P.L. or 42 U.S.C.§ 675(5)(H)) requires that within 90 days prior to ayoung adult being|**Transitional Living Plan (TLP)**<br>Foster Care 18-21 is a voluntary program. It would not be<br>appropriate to require young adults to partake in activities they<br>are not in agreementwith completing. It is not appropriate to<br>require participants to complete drug screens or participate in<br>therapy to engage in this program. If a young adult's substance<br>use or mental health impacts their eligibility, a plan should be<br>developed to support them and offer solutions that support<br>self-sufficiency, to include a harm reduction approach.<br>90 DAY TRANSITION PLAN<br>County CW workers can utilize Five Wishes as a tool to support<br>Healthcare Power of Attorney decisions at:<br>https://www.fivewishes.org/|

|**Transition Support Team (TST)**|**Transition Support Team (TST)**<br>A TST can be a formal meeting, should that be the desire of the<br>young adult. These meetings should be about ensuring the<br>young adult has the support needed toprogress on their stated|

|Purpose: The Transition Support Team (TST) exists to provide support to the young adult in<br>developing and achieving the goals identified on his or her Transitional Living Plan. During<br>these meetings, the support team must:<br>• Ensure the young adult has a sufficient social support system in place;<br>• Review the short and long-term goals identified by the young adult and<br>support team, and discuss action steps needed for the young adult to<br>achieve those goals;<br>• Discuss young adult's willingness to actively participate in an education<br>program and/or employment in order to meet eligibility requirements;<br>• Development of a backup plan in the event of unexpected breaks in<br>participation;<br>• Other factors affecting the young adult's transition to independence; and<br>• Any issues/concerns the young adult would like to discuss.<br>Young adults have the right to participate fully in every Transition Support Team meeting,<br>and the meetings should be scheduled with consideration of the young adult's schedule. A<br>timely written notice must be sent to the young adult, and other participants, prior to each<br>meeting that includes the date, time and location of the meeting. Signatures of all persons<br>attending the TST meeting must be recorded on the Transitional Living Plan.<br>Required TST Participants:<br>The following are required participants of a young adult's support team:<br>• Young Adult<br>• Caseworker<br>• At least one person the young adult identifies as a support<br>• Other participants may include, but are not limited to:<br>o Service providers<br>o Placement resource representative, if applicable<br>o Other individuals the young adult identifies<br>Note: The young adult must agree to each participant being a part of their support team.<br>In addition, the young adult must provide consent for their information to be shared with<br>each participant. The county CW is responsible for ensuring an active Release of<br>Information is on file for each participant.|goals. Meetings cannot be held without the young adult<br>present and should be held in the least-restrictive setting<br>possible.<br>It is important to prioritize scheduling around the young<br>person's school/work/volunteer schedule when possible and|

|Required Timeframes for TST Meetings<br>TST meetings must occur:<br>• Within 30 days of the young adult turning 18 years of age, but prior to the initial<br>court hearing; OR if a re-entry, within 30 days of the young adult signing the<br>VPA and 90 days from that date;<br>• Every three months thereafter;<br>• No more than 90 days prior to the young adult's 21st birthday, or planned exit<br>from Foster Care 18 to 21;<br>• Whenever there is a significant change in circumstances, or a break in<br>participation.<br>Note: Young adults can request to meet with their Transition Support Team more frequently<br>than required.<br>Review of Program Eligibility<br>Once program eligibility conditions have been established, the county CWA is<br>responsible for ensuring the young adult continues to meet the eligibility requirements<br>on an ongoing basis. This is done through monthly contacts with the young adult.<br>Information obtained is documented in the DSS 5098: NC Monthly Contact Record for<br>Foster Care 18 to 21. Written verification supporting the young adult's eligibility must<br>be reviewed and documented in the record at a minimum of every three months.<br>The court must review cases in which the young adult fails to meet one of the eligibility<br>requirements in N.C.G.S. § 108A-48(c) for 60 or more consecutive days to determine if<br>services should be terminated. In addition, young adults must immediately contact the<br>county CWA concerning any changes that would affect their eligibility such as the loss of<br>a job, withdrawal from school, or completion of a program.|hold the meetings in a location comfortable for the young<br>person and provides an atmosphere that supports<br>confidentiality.<br>The caseworker should coordinate transportation for the young<br>person to attend TST meetings.|

|**Temporary Breaks/Absences in Participation**|**Temporary Breaks/Absences in Participation**|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

|Young adults in the Foster Care 18 to 21 program may experience temporary absences<br>from their placement or short-term breaks in meeting eligibility requirements. These<br>situations do not automatically result in loss of services, provided certain conditions are<br>met.<br>Temporary Absences from Placement<br>A temporary absence from placement occurs when a young adult leaves their approved<br>living arrangement for a short period but intends to return. Examples include:<br>• Short-term hospitalizations<br>• Family visits<br>• Vacations<br>During such absences, Title IV-E Foster Care Maintenance payments may continue as long as<br>the absence does not exceed 14 days. If the absence extends beyond 14 days, the agency<br>must determine whether to suspend or terminate services based on the circumstances.<br>Temporary Breaks in Participation<br>Young adults may also experience temporary gaps in meeting eligibility requirements, such<br>as:<br>• Losing a job<br>• Taking a break from school due to medical or personal reasons<br>These situations do not automatically disqualify a young adult from the program. As long as<br>they are actively working toward the goals outlined in their Transitional Living Plan (TLP),<br>they may remain eligible. The TLP must include a fully developed backup plan to address<br>unexpected interruptions.<br>Acceptable activities during these breaks may include:<br>• Participation in programs that promote employment or remove barriers to<br>employment<br>• Recovery from medical conditions or disabilities that temporarily prevent meeting<br>education or work requirements|If the extended absence is approved by the county CWA. Foster<br>Care 18 to 21 services are temporarily suspended; however,<br>foster care maintenance payments may continue.<br>SFHF can be used to cover the number of days the young adult is<br>absent beyond 14-days and up to 30-days.|
|---|---|
