| Note: Scheduled school breaks (e.g., summer or holiday breaks) do not affect eligibility, as<br>long as the young adult is enrolled for the upcoming semester.<br>60-Day Grace Period<br>Young adults may continue receiving benefits and services for up to 60 days following a<br>break in participation. During this time, they must make reasonable efforts to meet at least<br>one eligibility requirement. If the 60-day period is nearing its end and the young adult is not<br>making sufficient progress, a Transition Support Team (TST) meeting must be held to:<br>• Develop a plan of action<br>• Determine whether the case should be reviewed in court<br>• Decide if termination of services is appropriate | |
|---|---|
|**Suspension Versus Termination of Services**<br>Suspension of Services: A suspension of services occurs when the young adult has been<br>away from his or her placement for more than 14 days with a valid explanation and has<br>communicated his or her intent to return to the program. In addition, the young adult<br>continues to meet at least one eligibility requirement for Foster Care 18 to 21 services. The<br>extended absence must be approved by the county CWA. Foster Care 18 to 21 services are<br>temporarily suspended, however, foster<br>care maintenance payments may continue. SFHF can be used to cover the amount of days<br>the young adult is absent beyond 14 days and up to 30 days.<br>Termination of Services:Termination of services occurs when the young adult has been<br>away from his or her placement for 14 or more days without approval from the county<br>CWA. In addition, the young adult has not communicated his or her intent to return to the<br>placement at a maximum of 14 days from the last date of attempted communication<br>between the county CWA and the young adult.<br>Foster care maintenance payments must be terminated immediately, and a court review<br>hearing must be held to terminate Foster Care 18 to 21 services.<br>**Termination Reasons**<br>Foster Care 18 to 21 services must be terminated when:|Suspension Versus Termination of Services|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 219 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 227 -->
|• The young adult reaches 21 years of age; or<br>• The young adult no longer meets the eligibility criteria; or<br>• The young adult requests that services be terminated; or<br>• The court has determined: the young adult is not meeting the goals of the<br>Transitional Living Plan and/or the young adult has violated the Voluntary<br>Placement Agreement for Foster Care 18 to 21; or<br>• The young adult has been absent from his / her approved placement for more<br>than 14 days without prior approval from the county department of social<br>services, and the court has terminated services.<br>In the event the young adult is refusing to live in an approved placement, is not making<br>reasonable efforts to meet the goals on the Transitional Living Plan, refuses to participate in<br>the development of the Transitional Living Plan or participate in Transition Support Team<br>Meetings, demonstrates an ongoing pattern of criminal activity, or has violated the<br>Voluntary Placement Agreement, the county CWA must request that the case be heard in<br>court to determine whether or not services should be terminated. This should only be<br>done after the county CWA has made reasonable efforts to engage the young adult in<br>services.<br>In addition, if the young adult no longer meets the eligibility criteria, he or she is allowed<br>a 60-day grace period in which reasonable efforts to achieve eligibility must be made on<br>the young adult's behalf, with the assistance of the county CWA. A transition between<br>eligibility requirements does not automatically disqualify young adults from the program,<br>however, if the 60-day transition period is coming to an end and the young adult has not<br>made reasonable efforts to meet eligibility requirements, the county CWA must request<br>the court to review the case and determine if the young adult's services should be<br>terminated.|Should the county CWA motion the court to review the<br>Voluntary Placement Agreement of a young adult, as per § 7B-<br>910.1.(d), a young adult must be provided with 15 days advance<br>written notice.|

|**Case Closure Notice**<br>Any time Foster Care 18 to 21 services are terminated by the court, a 30-day written<br>notice of termination of services must be sent to the young adult noting that their Foster<br>Care 18 to 21 benefits and services will terminate 30 days from the date of the notice.||

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

| The young adult has the right to motion the court to review the case if he or she<br>disagrees with the decision to terminate services. A Motion for Review (AOC-J-140) form<br>must be attached to the 30-day notice, and can be found at the following link.<br>Instructions on how to motion the court for review must be included in the written notice.<br>After termination, young adults can later re-enter the Foster Care 18 to 21 program at any<br>time prior to their 21st birthday, as long as one of the eligibility requirements in N.C.G.S. §<br>108A-48(c) is met.<br>NOTE: Medicaid must be notified whenever Foster Care 18 to 21 services have been<br>terminated. | |

|**Re-Entry Into Foster Care**<br>A young adult who was in foster care upon his or her 18th birthday may re-enter Foster<br>Care 18 to 21 any time before reaching 21 years of age. Upon exiting foster care at age 18 or<br>older, the young adult must be provided information on how to enter the Foster Care 18 to<br>21 program at a later date. If the young adult does choose to return, he or she may contact<br>the county CWA that previously held custody, or the agency in the county where the young<br>adult is currently residing. See Intercounty Collaboration.<br>The re-entry is considered a new foster care episode and eligibility determination is<br>required. The county CWA must verify program eligibility as well as determine funding<br>eligibility.<br>In order to re-enter Foster Care 18 to 21, the young adult must meet all of the criteria<br>stated in this policy:<br>•<br>The young adult is 18, 19, or 20 years old;<br>•<br>The young adult was in foster care upon his or her 18th birthday;<br>•<br>The young adult meets at least one of the eligibility requirements for Foster<br>Care 18 to 21;<br>•<br>The young adult is willing to enter into a Voluntary Placement Agreement and<br>abide by the provisions of the agreement; and<br>•<br>Theyoung adult agrees to reside in an approvedplacement.||

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 221 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 229 -->
| If the young adult meets the above conditions, the county CWA and the young adult must<br>enter into a Voluntary Placement Agreement for Foster Care 18 to 21, and the agreement<br>is effective the date it is signed. A Transition Support Team meeting must be held within<br>30 days of entering into the VPA and the Transitional Living Plan must be updated at that<br>time. A court hearing must be held within 90 days of the date the VPA is signed. | |

|**Adult Adoptions**<br>NC's adoption law does not restrict adoption to children. Adults may also be adopted. For<br>purposes of the adoption law, an adult is defined as an individual who is at least 18 years<br>old, or, if under the age of 18, is either legally married or has been emancipated under<br>applicable state law.<br>County CW agencies are responsible for facilitating the adult adoption of a young adult<br>receiving Foster Care 18 to 21 services in accordance with the provisions of N.C.G.S. §48,<br>Article 5. For more information on adult adoptions see the_Adoptions_section of this<br>manual or visit the NC CWAdoptions Manual.|A participant of the Foster Care 18-21 program may be adopted<br>as an adult, as their adoption status does not change the<br>eligibility criteria that they aged out of foster care.|
|**Health And Safety Standards for Semi-Supervised Independent Living**<br>**Arrangements**<br>The following minimum standards regarding health and safety must be met when<br>assessing semi-supervised independent living arrangements:<br>•<br>The young adult must have an appropriate sleeping area with a bed and room to<br>store his or her belongings. The sleeping area has no more than two adults and is<br>not a kitchen or bathroom.<br>•<br>The young adult must have access to a functional bathroom that contains at least<br>one toilet, one sink, and one tub or shower maintained in safe, operating condition<br>free from health hazards.||

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

Page 222 Return to Permanency Planning TOC Return to Manual TOC

<!-- page: 230 -->
| • The young adult must have an area to prepare meals, with adequate storage for<br>food free from health hazards. Appliances are in safe operating condition, if<br>applicable.<br>• Waste is stored, located and disposed of in a manner that will not permit the<br>transmission of communicable disease or odors, create a nuisance, or provide a<br>breeding place of food source for insects or rodents.<br>• Home has adequate and functioning ventilation including heating systems,<br>has running water and electricity.<br>• Lighting and outlets are provided in rooms used by the young adult and no<br>electrical hazards are present.<br>• Home has a functioning smoke detector installed near the young adult's sleeping<br>area and is audible in each room used by the young adult.<br>• The home has at least one exit that ensures a safe, direct, emergency exit to the<br>outside.<br>• Indoor and outdoor halls, stairs, ramps and porches are free from obstructions and<br>no structural damage that poses a safety hazard is observed. Living space appears<br>to be safe and free from hazards. | |

|**Intercounty Collaboration**<br>Young adults receiving Foster Care 18 to 21 services may choose to reside out-of- county<br>for education, employment, access to their support system, or any other reason. The<br>placement county must continue to provide case management and supervision. County<br>CW agencies can agree to courtesy supervision and must develop an Intercounty<br>Agreement for the Provision of Foster Care 18 to 21 Services (addendum to the Voluntary<br>Placement Agreement for Foster Care 18 to 21). Foster Care 18 to 21 cases can be<br>transferred to the county in which the young adult resides if it is in the best interest of the<br>young adult to do so, and both counties involved agree to the transfer.<br>Young adults who reside in a county other than the one that he or she was in foster care as<br>a minor, can contact either county to request to enter the Foster Care 18 to 21program.|**Intercounty Collaboration**<br>It is the professional discretion of the county that was<br>approached first to balance a young adult's established rapport<br>with their county of origin against their need for local support<br>from the county of residence to identify resources.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL

| The county that is contacted by the young adult must initiate Foster Care 18 to 21 services<br>by entering into a Voluntary Placement Agreement with the young adult within 14 days of<br>initial contact and documentation of eligibility. In these instances, both counties, the<br>county of origin as well as the county the young adult resides in, must develop an<br>Intercounty Agreement for the Provision of Foster Care Services (addendum to the<br>Voluntary Placement Agreement for Foster Care 18 to 21) at the time the Voluntary<br>Placement Agreement is signed. This should be a partnership driven plan in which the<br>young adult is involved in the planning process along with the two counties. The<br>agreement must outline what each county will be responsible for and must be signed by<br>each party involved. Counties must take the young adult's best interest into consideration<br>when developing the agreement. | |

|**Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children (ICPC) And Foster Care 18 to 21**<br>To support young adults who relocate to another state, county child welfare agencies may<br>submit an ICPC request to the state where the young adult is moving. The caseworker must<br>coordinate with the receiving state's monitoring worker to ensure the young person is<br>linked to the independent living program. If the ICPC is approved, monthly face-to-face<br>contacts will be required.<br>The local county CWA should complete a virtual visit within 14 days of the young adult's<br>move to assess the placement. An in-person visit must be completed within 30 days of the<br>virtual visit by the county of residence or the county of origin. The county of origin must<br>ensure an in-person visit to the home of the young person is completed at a minimum of<br>once every 90 days. The county of origin must visit the home of the young person directly<br>at a minimum of once every six months.<br>Additionally, NC accepts ICPC requests from other states and counties outside of NC for the<br>18-21 program. Implementation of an ICPC placement must comply with the ICPC<br>Compact. If an ICPC case is assigned to NC, the county DSS caseworker is required to<br>complete the supervision monthly contacts, assuring majority are face-to-face.|**Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children (ICPC) And**<br>**Foster Care 18 to 21**<br>For young adults who have aged out of foster care from another<br>state and approach a county department of social services with<br>the request to join Foster Care 18-21, all efforts should be made<br>to connect them to their state of origin to support their<br>participation in their state's extended foster care program.<br>Although optional,counties are highly encouraged to<br>collaborate with the state of origin to ensure the young adult is<br>receiving independent living services.<br>The Interstate Compact on the Placement of Children<br>regulates the interstate movement of children to ensure<br>protection and services when placed across state lines for<br>foster care and adoption purposes. Some states' age of<br>majority are defined differently from the Compact. This<br>leaves individual states to determine their age of majority<br>and their services that support young adults from other<br>states. As such, some states laws do not support placement<br>supervision or services to young adults in extended foster<br>care who move across state lines.|

Permanency Planning Services Policy, Protocol, and Guidance (April 2026) NC CHILD WELFARE MANUAL
